<!-- proof-7: body_md -->
For simplicity, we specialize to the case $d_V = 1$. If for sufficiently large $T$, $\max_j \|M_j\|_{\infty \to \infty} \leq c$, we have that 
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\begin{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
        (\Gamma^{(j)})^{-1} &= (D_j I + M)^{-1} =  (I - D_j^{-1}M + (D_j^{-1}M)^2 - \cdots) D_j^{-1}
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\end{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
    since $\|D_j^{-1}M_j\|_{\infty\to\infty} < 1$. Thus, taking norms of both sides, we get
    \[
        \|(\Gamma^{(j)})^{-1}\|_{\infty\to\infty} \leq c_1^{-1} \frac{1}{1-c_1^{-1}\|M\|_{\infty\to\infty}} \leq \frac{1}{c_1 - c}
    \]
    but as mentioned in the discussion after Condition \ref{con:inversematrix}, we are done. The ``in particular'' part follows from the inequalities (see \cite{HJ} Theorem 5.6.26):
    \[
        \| A \|_{\infty \to \infty} \leq \sqrt T \|A\| \quad \text{ and } \quad \|A\|_{\infty \to \infty} \leq T \|A\|_\infty.
    \]
<!-- end proof-7 -->
<!-- theorem-7: body_md -->
\label{thm:Mestimation2}
    Let $\Lambda_\ast(\delta)\geq J_\ast(\delta)\lor \delta$ be some upper bound. Suppose that $\Lambda_\ast(\delta)/\delta^2$ decreases as $\delta$ increases. There exists a constant $c$ depending only on $M$, such that for
    \[
        \sqrt N \delta_N^2 \geq c \Lambda_\ast(\delta_N), 
    \]
    we have for all $\delta \geq \delta_N$, 
    \[
        \mathbb P\big(\tau^2(\hat g_N|g_0) \geq 2(\tau^2(g_\ast|g_0)+\delta^2) | z_1, \dots z_N\big) \leq c\exp[-\frac{N\delta^2}{c^2}].
    \]
<!-- end theorem-7 -->
<!-- proof-8: body_md -->
The proof is the same as Theorem 2.1 in \cite{vdG2001}, except that instead of using Corollary 8.4 in \cite{vdG2000}, we use Lemma 3.2 in the same reference. To satisfy the hypothesis of Lemma 3.2, note that $\epsilon = (\epsilon_i)_{i}$ is an $N(0, \Gamma)$ random variable, and hence for $\gamma \in \mathbb R^N$, $\gamma^T\epsilon \sim N(0, \gamma^T\Gamma\gamma)$, so by the standard tail bound for a Gaussian, 
    \[
        \mathbb P\left(\sum_{i = 1}^N \epsilon_i\gamma_i \geq a\right) \leq \exp\left(-\frac{a^2}{2\gamma^T\Gamma\gamma}\right) \leq \exp\left(-\frac{a^2}{2\|\Gamma\||\gamma|^2}\right).
    \]
    Then the rest of the proof continues as normal.
<!-- end proof-8 -->
<!-- proof-9: body_md -->
The proof is similar to that of Proposition B.7 in \cite{NW24}. We use a slightly modified version of Theorem 2.1 in \cite{vdG2001}, see \textsection \ref{sec:MEstResult} for details. We note that the empirical norm $\| \cdot\|_{(N)}$ is bounded by $\| \cdot \|_\infty$, and is further bounded by the metric entropy of a ball of radius $m$ in $H^{a, a/2}$, which by Corollary \ref{cor:metricentropy} has metric entropy of order $H(\tau) \lesssim (m/\tau)^{(d+2)/a}$ for every $m >0$. To control the bias term, we first observe that for $\theta\in \Theta_N$, we have $\theta = \theta_1 + \theta_2$, with $\|\theta_1\|_{(H^{\kappa}(\mathcal Z))^\ast}\leq M\varepsilon_N$ and $\|\theta_2\|_{\mathcal H} \leq M$, $\|\theta\|_{\mathcal R}\leq M$ for some $M = M(B)$.
    By Condition \ref{con:forwardmapping}, we have that there is some $U = U(B)$ such that $\|u_\theta\|_{H^{6, 3}} \leq U$ and $\|u_{\theta_2}\|_{H^{6, 3}} \leq U$ and $\|u_{\theta_2}\|_{H^{a, a/2}} \leq U$. The bias term is then
    \[
        \|u_\theta - u_{\theta_2, L}\|_{(N)} \leq \|u_\theta - u_{\theta_2}\|_{(N)} + \|u_{\theta_2} - u_{\theta_2, L}\|_{(N)}.
    \]
    We next make use Lemma \ref{lem:deterministicMconcentration} with $v = u_\theta - u_{\theta_2}$ and $u_{\theta_2} - u_{\theta_2, L}$ to specialize to the event 
    \[
    A_N = \big\{ |\|v\|_{(N)}^2 - \|v\|_{L^2}^2| \leq \lambda \delta_N^2 \text{ for } v = u_\theta - u_{\theta_2}, u_{\theta_2} - u_{\theta_2, L}\big\},
    \]
   which has $\mathbb P_\theta^N(A_N) \geq 1 - \exp(-KN\delta_N^2)$ for $\lambda$ large enough. (Note that the event $A_N$ depends only on the design $(t_i, X_{ij})_{ij}$.) Then, we use \eqref{eq:biascontrol2} to see on $A_N$,
    \[
        \|u_\theta - u_{\theta_2, L} \|_{(N)} \leq \|u_\theta - u_{\theta_2}\|_{L^2} + C\delta_N,
    \]
    but now we use Condition \ref{con:forwardregularity} and $\|\theta_1\|_{(H^\kappa(\mathcal Z))^\ast} \leq M\varepsilon_N$ to get that 
    \[
        \|u_\theta - u_{\theta_2, L} \|_{(N)} \leq C(\varepsilon_N + \delta_N).
    \]
We finish by applying Theorem \ref{thm:Mestimation2} on all the designs $(t_i, X_{ij})_{ij}$ belonging to $A_N$.
<!-- end proof-9 -->
<!-- lemma-5: body_md -->
\label{lem:deterministicMconcentration}
    In the setting of \eqref{eq:timedependent} and \eqref{eq:setting}, suppose that $v = v_N \in H^{6, 3}\cap L^\infty$ is a (deterministic) sequence with $\|v_N\|_{H^{6, 3}}\leq U$ and $\|v_N\|_\infty\leq U$ for all $N$, and suppose that 
    \[
        n\lesssim N\delta_N\text{ and } T \gtrsim \delta_N^{-1},
    \]
    as well as $\|v_N\|_{L^\infty_tL^2_x} \lesssim \delta_N$.
    Then for any $K > 0$, there is a $\lambda(U)  > 0$ so that 
    \[
        \mathbb P^N_\theta\big( |\|v \|_{(N)}^2 - \|v \|_{L^2}^2 | \leq \lambda\delta_N^2\big) \geq 1 - e^{-KN\delta_N^2}.
    \]
<!-- end lemma-5 -->
<!-- proof-10: body_md -->
First, consider the collection of independent, mean-zero random variables 
    \[
        |v(t_i, X_{ij})|^2  - \|v(t_i, \cdot)\|^2_{L^2(\Omega)} \quad \text{ for }i = 1, \dots, T, j = 1, \dots, n
    \]
    Since $\|v\|_\infty \leq U$, this is a collection of independent random variables which are uniformly bounded and have mean zero. Also, we have that 
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {

    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
        \operatorname{Var}(|v(t_i, X_{ij})|^2) &\leq \mathbb E^N(|v(t_i, X_{ij})|^4) \leq U^2\mathbb E^N(|v(t_i, X_{ij})|^2) \leq U^2\delta_N^2.
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {

        {

        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
    Thus, applying Bernstein's inequality, we obtain
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\begin{align*}}

    }

    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
        \mathbb P^N_\theta \left(\left|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i, j} |v(t_i, X_{ij})|^2 -\frac{1}{T}\sum_{i=1}^T \|v(t_i, \cdot) \|^2_{L^2(\Omega)} \right| \geq \lambda \delta_N^2\right) &\leq 2 \exp\left(\frac{-N^2\delta_N^4\lambda^2}{2(NU^2\delta_N^2 + \frac{1}{3}U^2N\delta_N^2\lambda)}\right)\\
        &\leq 2\exp\left( - \frac{\lambda^2}{2U^2(1 + \lambda/3)} N\delta_N^2\right).
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {

    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
    Hence, taking $\lambda \geq 0$ large enough, we get
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {

        \mathbb P^N_\theta\left(\left|  \|v\|^2_{(N)} - \frac{1}{T}\sum_{i = 1}^T\|v(t_i, \cdot) \|^2_{L^2(\Omega)} \right| \leq \lambda \delta_N^2 \right) \geq 1 - \exp\left( -K N\delta_N^2\right).

    \[
        t \mapsto \|v(t, \cdot)\|_{L^2(\Omega)}^2
    \]
    is $C^2([0, 1])$ with its norm depending only on $U$. Now, consider

<!-- lemma-6: body_md -->
\label{lem:approximationtheory}
       Now, we know that for $g\in C^2([0, 1])$, and for $t_i = (2i-1)/2T$, $i = 1, \dots T$,  we have
       \[
           \left|\frac{1}{T}\sum_{i = 1}^T g(t_i) - \int_0^1 g(t) \, dt \right| \leq \frac{ \| g \|_{C^2}}{24T^2}.
       \]
<!-- end lemma-6 -->
<!-- lemma-7: body_md -->
\label{lem:spatialconcentration}
    With the setup from Proposition \ref{prop:Mestimation}, assume that $a > (d+1)/2$, and suppose that
    \[
        n \gtrsim N^{\frac{2d+3}{2a + d + 2}} \quad \text{ and }\quad T \lesssim N^{\frac{2a - d - 1}{2a + d +2}}.
    \]
    Then there exists a sequence $\gamma_N$ so that $N\delta_N^2 = o(\gamma_N)$ and for every $i = 1, \dots T$,
    \[
        \mathbb P^N_\theta\left(\left| \frac{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j =1}^n |(\hat u - u_{\theta, L})(t_i, X_{ij})|^2}{\|(\hat u - u_{\theta, L})(t_i, \cdot ) \|^2_{L^2(\Omega)}} - 1\right|\leq \frac{1}{2}\right) \geq 1 - c'e^{-\gamma_N},
    \]
    where $u_{\theta, L}$ is as in \eqref{eq:projectionoftruth}, and $c$ is some positive constant.
<!-- end lemma-7 -->
<!-- proof-11: body_md -->
We know that both $\hat u (t_i, \cdot)$ and $u_{\theta, L}(t_i, \cdot)$ are in $\mathbf V_{L+1}$ (because every spatial wavelet in the sum for fixed $t$ has level $\leq L$)\footnote{See \textsection \ref{sec:WaveletFacts} for details}. Hence, we can apply the argument from step IV in Proposition B.7 in \cite{NW24}, for $\Gamma = I$, and $\hat \Gamma$ being
    \[
        \hat \Gamma_{kk'} = \frac{1}{n}\sum_{j = 1}^n \Phi_{Lk}(X_{ij})\Phi_{Lk'}(X_{ij}),
    \]
    where $\Phi^{\iota}_{Lk}$ are tensorized Meyer scaling functions, and we note that $\hat \Gamma$ is a $2^{(L+1)d} \times 2^{(L+1)d}$ matrix. Then, we let $M_L$ be the covering number of a ball of radius $\kappa$ for some fixed $\kappa^2 + 2\kappa < 1/4$ in $\mathbb R^{2^{(L+1)d}}$, with $\{ v_1, \dots, v_{M_L}\}$ the covering vectors, and we define $U \equiv c N^{d/(2a + d +2)} $. Then, arguing as in \cite{NW24}, we deduce
    \[
        M_J \lesssim e^{c_0 2^{Ld}} \leq e^{c_1 N^{d/(2a + d + 2)}} 
    \]
    where the constants are absolute. 
    Finally, in the application of Bernstein's inequality, we get
    \[
        \mathbb P(n|v_m^T(\hat \Gamma - I)v_m| > n/4) \leq 2 \exp\left(-\frac{n^2/16}{2nU + (2/12)nU}\right) \leq 2 e^{-c_0n/U}
    \]
    Now, we now that $n/U\gtrsim N^{\frac{d+3}{2a + d + 2}},$ and hence we can bound
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\begin{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
        \mathbb P \left( \max_{m = 1, \dots, M_J} |v^T_m (\hat \Gamma - I)v_m| \geq 1/4\right) &\leq M_J \max_m \mathbb P ( |v_m^T(\hat\Gamma - I)v_m| > 1/4) \\
        & \leq 2e^{c_1 N^{d/(2a + d +2)} - c_0N^{(d+3)/(2a + d + 2)}} \\
        &\leq c'e^{-c'' N^{(d+2.5)/(2a + d +2)}} = c'e^{-\gamma_N}.
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\end{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
    This probability bounds the complement of the probability in question, so we are done.
<!-- end proof-11 -->
<!-- proof-12: body_md -->
We observe from Lemma \ref{lem:spatialconcentration} and a union bound that 
    \[
        \mathbb P^N_\theta\left(\left| \frac{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j =1}^n |(\hat u - u_{\theta, L})(t_i, X_{ij})|^2}{\|(\hat u - u_{\theta, L})(t_i, \cdot ) \|^2_{L^2(\Omega)}} - 1\right|\leq \frac{1}{2} \text{ for }i = 1, \dots T\right) \geq 1 - Tc'e^{-\gamma_N}.
    \]
    But since $T$ grows algebraically in $N$, and $N\delta_N^2 = o(\gamma_N)$, for sufficiently large $N \geq N_0$ we have after averaging over the $t_i$,
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        {\begin{align}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
        \mathbb P^N_\theta\bigg(\frac{1}{T}\sum_{i=1}^T\|(\hat u - u_{\theta, L})(t_i, \cdot ) \|^2_{L^2(\Omega)} \leq \frac{2}{Tn}\sum_{i = 1}^T&\sum_{j =1}^n |(\hat u - u_{\theta, L})(t_i, X_{ij})|^2 \bigg) \geq 1 - \frac{1}{2}e^{-KN\delta_N^2}.\label{eq:timeaveragebound}
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        {\end{align}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
    Next, from Proposition \ref{prop:Mestimation} by taking $c$ large enough, we have 
    \[
        \mathbb P^N_\theta( \|\hat \beta \|_{h^a}^2 \leq c | ((t_i, X_{ij}))_{ij}) \geq 1 - \frac{1}{2}e^{-KN\delta_N^2}.
    \]
    But we've defined $h^a$ so that $\| \hat \beta \|_{h^a} \asymp \| \hat u \|_{H^{a, a/2}}$, and since $a \geq 6$, we have $\|\hat u\|_{H^{6, 3}} \leq \|\hat u\|_{H^{a, a/2}}$.
    We know that additionally $u_{\theta, L} \in H^{6, 3}$ with $\| u_{\theta, L} \|_{H^{6, 3}}\leq U = U(B)$, so we know that
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {\begin{equation}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    \label{eq:hatinsobolevball}
        \mathbb P^N_\theta( \| \hat u - u_{\theta, L} \|^2_{H^{6, 3}} \leq 2c + 2U^2) \geq 1 - \frac{1}{2}e^{-KN\delta_N^2}.
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {\end{equation}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
    On this event, from the Sobolev characterization of $H^{6, 3}$, we get that $\hat u - u_{\theta, L} \in H^{3}([0, 1], L^2(\Omega))$, with $\|\hat u - u_{\theta, L}\|_{H^3_tL^2_x} \leq c_0$, where $c_0$ depends on $u_\theta$ only through $U$. Then, from \textsection 5.9.2 Theorem 3 in Evans, this means the function 
    \[
        t \mapsto \| \hat u - u_{\theta, L}(t, \cdot) \|_{L^2(\Omega)}^2
    \]
    is $C^2([0, 1])$ with its norm depending only on $c$ and $U$. Now, consider Lemma \ref{lem:approximationtheory} but with $g = \hat u - u_{\theta - L}$. Then, combining both \eqref{eq:timeaveragebound} and \eqref{eq:hatinsobolevball}, we get
    \[
        \mathbb P^N_\theta(\| \hat u - u_{\theta, L} \|_{L^2([0, 1]\times \Omega)}^2 \leq 2 \| \hat u - u_{\theta, L}\|_{(N)}^2 + \frac{c}{T^2}) \geq 1 -  e^{-K N\delta_N^2},
    \]
    as desired.
<!-- end proof-12 -->
<!-- proof-13: body_md -->
The map $DG$ we have defined is continuous and linear for any $(D, u) \in C^1(\Omega) \times F$.
    We can write
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\begin{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
        G(D+h, u+v) - G(D, u) &- DG(D, u)[h, v] \\
        &= \left(-\nabla \cdot(h\nabla v) - (f(u+v) - f(u) - f'(u)v), 0 \right),
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\end{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
    and we simply need so show the RHS is $o(\|h\|_{C^1} + \|v\|_F)$. Now, we have that
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\begin{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
        \|\nabla \cdot (h \nabla v ) \|_{L^2(Q)}&\leq \| \nabla h \cdot \nabla v \|_{L^2} + \|h\Delta v\|_{L^2} \\
                                                         &\leq d \|h\|_{C^1}\|v\|_{L^2_tH^1_x} + d\|h\|_\infty \|v\|_{L^2_tH^2_x}\\
                                                         &\leq 2d\|h\|_{C^1}\|v\|_F \leq d(\|h\|_{C^1} + \|v\|_F)^2,
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\end{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
    which bounds the first term in the sum. Next, note that from Taylor's theorem applied to $f$, for every $(t, x) \in Q$, there is some value $\xi( u, v) = \xi(u(t, x), v(t, x))$ such that
    \[
        f(u+v) = f(u) + f'(u)v + \frac{1}{2}f''(\xi(u, v))v^2.
    \]
    Hence, we have (also using the fact that $H^1(\Omega) \hookrightarrow L^4(\Omega)$ for $d\leq 4$):
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\begin{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
        \|f(u+v) - f(u) - f'(u)v\|_{L^2(Q)}^2 &= \int_{Q} \frac{1}{4} f''(\xi)^2 v^4\, dtdx \leq \frac{1}{4}\|f''\|_\infty^2 \int_0^1 \|v\|_{L^4}^4 \, dt\\
                                                       &\leq C \int_0^1 \|v\|_{H^1}^4\, dt \leq C \|v\|_{C([0, 1], H^1(\Omega))}^4.
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\end{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
    Thus, since $F \hookrightarrow C([0, 1], H^1(\Omega))$, we have
    \[
        \|f(u+v) - f(u) - f'(u)v\|_{L^2(Q)} \leq C\|v\|F^2 = o(\|h\|_{C^1} + \|v\|_F).
    \]
<!-- end proof-13 -->
<!-- proof-14: body_md -->
The first part follows from the remark above Proposition \ref{prop:HRDEForward}. Next, by the fundamental theorem of calculus for Fr\'echet derivatives in Banach spaces (see e.g. \cite{LR} Proposition A.2.3) we can write, for $D_1, D_2 \in U$, and $D_w = (1-w)D_1 + wD_2$, 
\[
    u_{D_2}-u_{D_1} = \int_0^1 D\mathcal G(D_w)[D_2 - D_1] \, dw,
\]
with convergence of the integral in $F$, and hence also in $L^\infty_tL^2_x$. Thus, by triangle inequality, 
\[
    \|u_{D_2} - u_{D_1} \|_{L^\infty_tL^2_x} \leq \int_0^1 \|D\mathcal G(D_w)[D_2 - D_1]\|_{L^\infty_tL^2_x}.
\]
But now we can estimate, by Proposition \ref{prop:LinearParabolicRegularity} and $L^2_tH^1_x \cap H^1_t H^{-1}_x \hookrightarrow C([0, 1], L^2(\Omega))$ (Theorem 3 in \textsection 5.9 in \cite {Evans}),
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\begin{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
    \|D\mathcal G(D_w)[D_2 - D_1]\|_{L^\infty_tL^2_x} &\lesssim \|\nabla \cdot ([D_2 - D_1]\nabla u_{D_w})\|_{L^2_t H^{-1}_x} \leq \| \nabla u_{D_w} \|_{L^\infty(Q)} \|D_2-D_1\|_{L^2(Q)}
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\end{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    

But now we apply Proposition \ref{prop:HRDEReg}, and since $\beta > d/2 + 1$ and $\nabla u \in H^{\beta, \beta/2}$, by Proposition \ref{prop:BesovEmbeddings}, $\nabla u \in L^\infty(Q)$. Note that the upper bound is uniform in $w$ as $\|D_w\|_{C^1} \leq U$, and $D_{\min}$ is the same for $D_1, D_2$ and $D_w$. Hence, 
\[
    \|u_{D_2} - u_{D_1} \|_{L^\infty_t L^2_x} \lesssim \|D_2 - D_1 \|_{L^2(Q)}.
\]
<!-- end proof-14 -->
<!-- proof-15: body_md -->
The proof of $(b)$ is the same as in \textsection 2.1.2.1 in \cite{N}, noting that we can extend $h$ to an $H^1(\Omega)$ function by $0$, and using periodic divergence theorem. The proof of $(a)$ uses a variant of Lemma 3.7 in \cite{ABJN}. To start, note that the second inequality is an application of H\"older's inequality. Also, write $u$ for $u_{D_1}$. For any $t \in [0, 1]$, we have from Lemma 3.6 in \cite{ABJN} that
    \[
        \| \nabla \cdot(h\nabla u(t, \cdot))\|_{L^2(\Omega)}\geq \| \nabla \cdot(|h|\nabla u(t, \cdot))\|_{L^2(\Omega)}.
    \]
    From Proposition \ref{prop:HRDEReg} we have $\|u\|_{H^{\beta + 1, (\beta+1)/2}} \leq C(M, D_{\min}, \beta, d, f)$. Now, we use the duality characterization of the $L^2$ norm:
    \[
        C\| \nabla \cdot(|h|\nabla u(t, \cdot))\|_{L^2(\Omega)} \geq \sup_{\|v\|_{L^2(\Omega)}\leq C}\int_\Omega v\nabla \cdot(|h|\nabla u(t, \cdot))\, dx
    \]
    Thus, choosing $v = -u$ and using the divergence theorem 
    \[
        \sup_{\|v\|_{L^2(\Omega)}\leq C}\int_\Omega v\nabla \cdot(|h|\nabla u(t, \cdot)\, dx \geq \int_{\Omega} |h| |\nabla u(t, \cdot)|^2 \, dx.
    \]
    Putting the chain of inequalities together, and integrating in time (note that $h$ is independent of time) yields the result.
<!-- end proof-15 -->
<!-- proof-16: body_md -->
We employ the idea of \textsection 2.2.2 in \cite{CN} on $G$ as defined in \eqref{eq:levelsetmap} to obtain

       &\int_0^1 \left( -\nabla \cdot([D_1 - D_2]\nabla u_{D_1}), 0 \right) \, dw \\
       &\quad = -\int_0^1 \left( \frac{\partial}{\partial t}[u_{1} - u_{2}] - \nabla \cdot (D_2 \nabla(u_{1} - u_{2})) - f'(\mathcal G_w)(u_{1} - u_{2}), 0\right)\, dw,

        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
   where $u_i = \mathcal G(D_i)$, and $\mathcal G_w = (1-w)u_1 + w u_2$.
   Hence, applying $L^2(Q)$ norms to both sides, and using that the RHS $\lesssim \|u_1 - u_2\|_{H^{2, 1}}$, we get 
   \[
       \| \nabla \cdot([D_1 - D_2]\nabla u_{D_1}) \|_{L^2(Q)} \lesssim \|u_1 -u_2\|_{H^{2, 1}}.
   \]
   Now, if Condition \ref{con:Darcy} holds, we have by Lemma \ref{lem:transportstability} for $h = D_1 - D_2$ that
   \[
       \|D_1 - D_2\|_{L^1(\Omega)} \lesssim \| \nabla \cdot([D_1 - D_2]\nabla u_{D_1}) \|_{L^2(Q)}.
   \]
   To finish, we use the following interpolation inequalities:\footnote{For interpolation between $H^{\beta, \beta/2}$ spaces, this follows from H\"older's inequality for sequence spaces. For Sobolev spaces, see p.44 in \cite{LM}.}

       \|u_1 - u_2\|_{H^{2, 1}} &\leq \|u_1 - u_2\|^{\frac{\beta-1}{\beta +1}}_{L^2} \|u_1 - u_2 \|^{\frac{2}{\beta + 1}}_{H^{\beta + 1, (\beta+1)/2}} \\
       \|D_1 - D_2\|_{L^2} &\lesssim \|D_1 - D_2\|_{H^{-d/2-1}}^{\frac{\beta}{\beta + d/2 + 1}}\|D_1 - D_2\|_{H^{\beta}}^{\frac{d/2 + 1}{\beta + d/2 + 1}}\lesssim \|D_1 - D_2\|_{L^1}^{\frac{\beta}{\beta + d/2 + 1}}

For $u_i = u_{\theta_i}$ and $w = u_1 - u_2$, $w$ satisfies (from subtracting the equations for $u_1, u_2$)

        w(0) &= \varphi_1 - \varphi_2.

    \[
        |\nu_1 - \nu_2| \leq C(M, d, \nu_{\min}, f) \|w\|_{H^{2, 1}}.
    \]
    From \cite{Evans} \textsection 5.9.2 Theorem 3, we have that

The idea is that if $Au$ starts out bounded below, then the integral will be bounded below, and when $Au$ is sufficiently small, the dynamics of the equation force $u$ to grow in the direction of $f$.

    We start by testing \eqref{eq:introNS} against the forcing function $f$ to obtain

    Rearranging this equation and applying Cauchy-Schwarz with the usual bounds on the bilinear form, we get

        \|f\|^2_{L^2}\leq \frac{d}{dt} \big[\langle u_\theta, f \rangle\big] + C(\|u_\theta\|_{H^1} + \|u_\theta\|^2_{H^1})\|f\|_{H^1} \text{ on }[0, 1].

    Integrate this equation from $s$ to $t$ ($0\leq s \leq t\leq 1$) to get

        (t-s)\|f\|^2_{L^2} &\leq \langle u_\theta(t), f\rangle_{L^2} - \langle u_\theta(s), f\rangle_{L^2} + C\|f\|_{H^1}\int_{s}^t \bigg(\|u_\theta\|_{H^1} + \|u_\theta\|^2_{H^1}\bigg) \\
        &\leq \|f\|_{L^2}\|u_\theta(t)\|_{L^2} + \|f\|_{L^2}\| u_\theta(s)\|_{L^2} + C\|f\|_{H^1}\int_{s}^t \bigg(\|u_\theta\|_{H^1} + \|u_\theta\|^2_{H^1}\bigg).

    Now we integrate $s$ from $0$ to $t$, obtaining

        &+ C\|f\|_{H^1} \int_0^t \, ds\int_s^t \bigg(\|u_\theta(\tau)\|_{H^1} + \|u_\theta(\tau)\|^2_{H^1}\bigg)\, d\tau.\label{eq:weirdintegral}

        &\leq t\int_0^t \bigg(\|u_\theta(\tau)\|_{H^1} + \|u_\theta(\tau)\|^2_{H^1}\bigg)\, d\tau.

        \frac{t^2}{2}\frac{\|f\|_{L^2}^2}{\|f\|_{H^1}} \leq C\bigg(t\|u_\theta(t)\|_{H^1}+ t\int_0^1 \|u_\theta(s) \|_{H^1}^2\, ds + (1+t)\int_0^1 \|u_\theta\|_{H^1}\, ds\bigg),

    and then we integrate this from $0$ to $1$ again and use Cauchy-Schwarz as well as the Poincar\'e inequality to get the result.

We know from Lemma 8.21 in \cite{GvdV} that
    \[
        \mathbb P^N_{\theta_0}\left( \int_\Theta \frac{p^N_\theta}{p^N_{\theta_0}}\, d\Pi_N(\theta) \leq \Pi_N(\mathcal B_N)e^{-2N\varepsilon_N^2}\right) \leq \frac{1}{N\varepsilon_N^2}.
    \]
    So if we consider events
    \[
        A_N = \{ \int_\Theta \frac{p^N_\theta}{p^N_{\theta_0}}\, d\Pi_N(\theta) \geq e^{-(A+2)N\varepsilon_N^2} \}, 
    \]
    then we see that $\mathbb P^N_{\theta_0}(A_N) \to 1$ as $N \to \infty$. Now, consider
    \[
        \bar \Theta_N = \Theta_N \cap \{ C\delta_N \geq  \|\mathcal G(\theta) - \mathcal G(\theta_0)\|_{L^2} \}.
    \]
    Then, by the definition of the posterior, and \eqref{eq:testcondition}, we have 
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align}

    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
        \mathbb P^N_{\theta_0}\big( \Pi(\bar \Theta_N^c|D_N)\geq e^{-bN\varepsilon_N^2}\big) &= \mathbb P_{\theta_0}^N\left( \frac{\int_{\bar \Theta_N^c}p^N_{\theta}/p^N_{\theta_0} \, d\Pi(\theta)}{\int_\Theta p^N_\theta/p^N_{\theta_0} \, d\Pi(\theta)} \geq e^{-bN\varepsilon_N^2}, \phi_N = 0, A_N\right) + o(1)\nonumber \\
        &\leq \mathbb P_{\theta_0}^N\left( \int_{\bar \Theta_N^c}p^N_{\theta}/p^N_{\theta_0} \, d\Pi(\theta) (1-\phi_N) \geq e^{-(b + A + 2)N\varepsilon_N^2}\right) + o(1).\label{eq:posteriorlowerbound}
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        {\end{align}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
    By Markov's inequality and Fubini's theorem, we can bound \eqref{eq:posteriorlowerbound} by
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {

        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*}

    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
        e^{(b+A + 2)N\varepsilon_N^2} \mathbb \int_{\bar \Theta_N^c}\mathbb E^N_{\theta_0}\big[\frac{p_\theta^N}{p_{\theta_0}^N}(1 - \phi_N)\big] \, d\Pi(\theta) \leq e^{(b+A + 2)N\varepsilon_N^2} \mathbb \int_{\bar \Theta_N^c}\mathbb E^N_{\theta}\big[(1 - \phi_N)\big] \, d\Pi(\theta).
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\end{align*}}

    Now, we use the fact $\bar \Theta_N^c = \Theta_N^c \cup \{\|\mathcal G(\theta) - \mathcal G(\theta_0)\|_{L^2} > C\delta_N\}$ and the bound on type two errors \eqref{eq:testcondition} to get

        \eqref{eq:posteriorlowerbound} &\leq e^{(b+A+2)N\varepsilon_N^2} \bigg(2 \Pi(\Theta_N^c) + \int_{\Theta_N \cap \{ C\delta_N < \| \mathcal G(\theta) - \mathcal G(\theta_0)\|_{L^2}\}} \hspace{-5mm}\mathbb E_\theta^N [ 1 -\phi_N] \, d\Pi(\theta)\bigg) \\
        &\lesssim e^{(b+A+2 - B)N\varepsilon_N^2} + e^{(b+A+2-K)N \varepsilon_N^2} \to 0,

    as $N \to \infty$.

<!-- proof-20: body_md -->
First, we show that all the critical points must move at some rate away from their original locations. For $\alpha > d/2 + 5$ the following computations are justified. Taking the gradient of \eqref{eq:introRDE}, we see
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\begin{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
        \nabla u_t &= \nabla(\nabla \cdot (D\nabla u)) + f'(u)\nabla u\\
                   &= \nabla D \Delta u + D\nabla \Delta u+\nabla^2 u\nabla D+\nabla^2 D \nabla u + f'(u)\nabla u,
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\end{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
    but if we evaluate this at the point $(0, x^\ast)$ for a critical point $x^\ast \in \Omega$ of $\varphi$, then we have $\nabla \varphi(x^\ast) = 0$, so the last two terms cancel, and we have
    \[
        \nabla u_t(0, x^\ast) = \nabla D\Delta\varphi + D \nabla \Delta\varphi + \nabla^2 \varphi \nabla D.
    \]
    Thus, by \eqref{eq:movingcriticalpoints}, we get that $|(d/dt)\nabla u(0, x^\ast)| \geq \delta$, so in particular for some $i = 1, \dots d$, $|\partial_t \partial_i u(0, x^\ast)| \geq \delta/d$. Now, using Proposition \ref{prop:HRDEReg}, we see
    \[
        \|\nabla u(\cdot, x^\ast)\|_{C^2([0, 1])}\leq C\|u\|_{H^{\alpha +1, \operatorname{an}}} \leq C(M, d, \alpha, D_{\min}, f).
    \]
    Now, we have from the mean-value theorem that there is some $0 < \Tilde t < t $ so that 
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\begin{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
        t\partial_t\partial_i u(\Tilde t, x^\ast)&= \partial_i u(t, x^\ast) - \partial_i u(0, x^\ast) \\
        &= \partial_i u(t, x^\ast).
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*}

    }

    So, $|\partial_t\partial_i u(\Tilde t, x^\ast) - \partial_t\partial_i u(0, x^\ast)| \leq t \|\nabla u(\cdot,x^\ast)\|_{C^2}\leq tC(M) $, and hence for $t < C(M)^{-1}\delta/(2d)$, we see
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\begin{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
        |\nabla u(t, x^\ast)| \geq |\partial_i u(t, x^\ast)| =  t|\partial_t \partial_i u(\Tilde t, x^\ast)| \geq t \frac{\delta}{2d},
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\end{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
    so, if we fix some $C(M)^{-1}\delta/(2d)> t^\ast >0$ (which depends only on $\delta, M, d$) we get for all $t^\ast < t < C(M)^{-1}\delta/(2d)$
    \[
        |\nabla u(t, x^\ast)| > c(\delta, d, M).
    \]
    Now, using the fact that the Lipschitz constant of $\nabla u$ is bounded by some constant depending on $M, d, D_{\min}, \alpha, f$, we see that there is some $\eta = \eta(\delta, d, M, D_{\min}, \alpha, f)$ so that for all $t^\ast < t < C(M)^{-1}\delta/(2d)$ and $|x - x^\ast| < \eta$, 
    \[
        |\nabla u(t, x)| > c(\delta, d, M)/2.
    \]
    Then, we can integrate this in time to get that
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {\begin{equation}}

        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    \label{eq:DarcyCritical}
        \int_0^1 |\nabla u(t, x)|^2 \, dt \geq c_1 >0
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {\end{equation}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
    for any $x$ within distance $\eta$ of a critical point $x^\ast$. Now, if we let 
    \[
        R = \{ x \in \Omega : \text{ for all critical points }x^\ast, |x - x^\ast| \geq \eta/2\},
    \]
    then we know that $|\nabla \varphi| > 0$ on $R$, and $R$ is compact, so $|\nabla \varphi|$ is uniformly lower bounded on $R$. Thus, utilizing the Lipschitz constant of $\nabla u$, we get that there is some constant $c_2 = c_2(M, d, D_{\min}, \alpha, f)$ such that
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {

    \label{eq:DarcyNonCritical}
        \int_0^1 |\nabla u(t, x)|^2 \, dt \geq c_2 >0 \quad \text{for all}\,\, x\in R.
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {

    }

    But combining \eqref{eq:DarcyCritical} and \eqref{eq:DarcyNonCritical} verifies Condition \ref{con:Darcy} for $c_0 = \min(c_1, c_2)$.

Note that by the triangle inequality, $D \geq D_{\min}$, and $\|\nabla D\|\leq M$, we have
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        {\begin{align}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
        |D\nabla(\Delta \varphi) + \Delta \varphi \nabla D + \nabla^2 \varphi \nabla D| &\geq |D| |\nabla(\Delta \varphi)| - |\Delta\varphi||\nabla D| - \|\nabla^2 \varphi\||\nabla D|\nonumber \\
                                                                                        &\geq D_{\min} |\nabla (\Delta \varphi)| - M(|\Delta \varphi| + \|\nabla^2 \varphi\|),\label{eq:criticallb}
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        {\end{align}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
    where $\|\nabla^2 \varphi\|$ is the operator norm. The idea is then to construct a function that has a mild second derivative, but large third derivative. We search for a function of the form 
    \[
        \varphi(x) = \sum_{i = 1}^d g(x_i).
    \]
    Then, we compute that
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        {\begin{align}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    \label{eq:varphiformula}
         
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        {\begin{split}} 
        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
        \nabla \varphi(x) &= (g'(x_1), \dots, g'(x_d))\\
        \nabla^2 \varphi (x) &= \operatorname{diag}(g''(x_1), \dots, g''(x_d)) \\
        \Delta \varphi(x) &= \sum_{i = 1}^d g''(x_i) \\
        \nabla \Delta \varphi(x) &= (g'''(x_1), \dots, g'''(x_d)).
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        {\end{split}} 
        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        {\end{align}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
    Thus, we get the bound
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {\begin{equation}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    \label{eq:criticallb2}
        (\ref{eq:criticallb}) \geq D_{\min} |\nabla \Delta \varphi| - 2dM\|g''\|_\infty,
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {\end{equation}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
    which needs to be greater than some $\delta$ at every critical point of $\varphi$. Now, consider the functions $g_n: \mathbb T \to \mathbb R$:
    \[
        g_n(x) = -\frac{1}{2\pi n^2} \cos(2\pi nx) + \frac{1}{4\pi n^2}\sin(4\pi nx).
    \]
    Then, 
    \[
        g_n'(x) = \frac{1}{n} \sin( 2\pi nx) + \frac{1}{n}\cos(4\pi nx).
    \]
    Then, using the double angle formula, we see that $g_n'(x_c) = 0$ if and only if
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\begin{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
        0 &= \sin(2 \pi nx_c) + 1 - 2\sin^2(2\pi nx_c) \\
        &= (1 + 2\sin(2\pi nx_c))(1 - \sin(2\pi nx_c)),
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\end{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
    so let $C_n$ be the set of points on $\mathbb T$ such that $\sin( 2\pi n x_c) = -1/2$ or $1$. Next, note that
    \[
        |g_n''(x)| = |2\pi\cos(2\pi n x) - 4\pi \sin(4\pi nx)| \leq 6\pi,
    \]
    so $\|g''\|_\infty \leq 6\pi$. Finally, we have
    \[
        g_n'''(x) = -4\pi^2 n \sin(2 \pi n x) - 16\pi^2 n\cos(4\pi nx).
    \]
    Now, at any critical point, we have $\sin(2 \pi n x_c) = 1/2$ or $-1$. But 
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\begin{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
        \sin(2\pi n x_c) = -1/2 &\implies \cos(4 \pi n x_c) = 1/2, \\
        \sin(2 \pi n x_c) = 1 &\implies \cos(4 \pi n x_c) = -1.
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\end{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
    Hence, $g_n'''(x_c) = -6\pi^2 n$ or $12\pi^2 n$. Now, fix an $n$ large enough so that $6\pi^2 n  D_{\min}\sqrt d - 12dM\pi > 1$, noting that $n$ depends only on $M, D_{\min}$, and $d$. Then, from \eqref{eq:varphiformula}, we see that the critical points of $\varphi$ with this choice of $g = g_n$ is $(C_n)^d\subseteq \Omega$, and hence for $x_c \in (C_n)^d$
    \[
        |\nabla \Delta \varphi(x_c)| \geq 6\pi^2 n\sqrt d,
    \]
    so by \eqref{eq:criticallb2} we are done.
<!-- end proof-21 -->
<!-- proof-22: body_md -->
We will show using Lemma \ref{lem:verifyingDarcy} that the set of $\varphi$ satisfying Condition \ref{con:Darcy} for some $c_0$ contains an open dense set. To start, set $L_D(\varphi) = D\nabla(\Delta \varphi) + \Delta \varphi \nabla D + \nabla^2 \varphi \nabla D$.
    Then, let $G$ be the set 
    \[
        G = \{ \varphi \in C^k(\Omega): \nexists x^\ast \in \Omega \text{ s.t. at } x^\ast,\, \nabla \varphi = 0 \text{ and } L_D(\varphi) = 0\}.
    \]
    First, we will show that $G^c$ is closed. Suppose that $\varphi_n\in G^c$ with $\varphi_n \to \varphi$ in $C^k$. Then, $\varphi_n \to \varphi$ in $C^3$. Also, there exists $x_n\in \Omega$ such that
    \[
        \nabla \varphi_n(x_n) = 0 \text{ and } L_D(\varphi_n)(x_n) = 0.
    \]
    But since $\Omega$ is compact, there exists a subsequence $n_k$ such that $x_{n_k} \to x$ for some $x\in \Omega$. But since $\varphi_n\to \varphi$ and $L_D(\varphi_n)\to L_D(\varphi)$ uniformly as $n\to \infty$, we have 
    \[
        \nabla \varphi(x) = 0 \text{ and } L_D(\varphi)(x) = 0,
    \]
    and hence $\varphi \in G^c$ so $G$ is open. Now we show that $G$ is dense. First, let $\varphi \in C^k$ and $\epsilon > 0$. Then, by Theorem 6.1.2 in \cite{H} there exists some $\Tilde \varphi\in C^k$ such that $\|\varphi - \Tilde \varphi \|_{C^k} < \epsilon/2$ and $\Tilde \varphi$ is Morse, i.e. has isolated critical points, all of which are non-degenerate. Since $\Omega$ is compact there can only be finitely many of them.

    Let $x_1, \dots, x_m$ be the set of points for which
    \[
        \nabla \Tilde \varphi(x_i) = 0 \text{ and } L_D(\Tilde \varphi)(x_i) = 0.
    \]
    Now, by translation and Proposition \ref{prop:RDvarphi}, we know there exists $g_i\in C^\infty$ such that 
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {\begin{equation}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    \label{eq:fixingfunction}
        \nabla g_i(x_i) = 0 \text{ and } L_D(g_i)(x_i) \neq 0
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {\end{equation}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
    By multiplying by a cutoff function $\equiv 1$ on a neighborhood of $x^\ast$ and $\equiv 0$ around all other critical points, we can also assume that the support of each $g_i$ only contains a single critical point of $\Tilde \varphi$ (namely $x_i$). Then consider the function 
    \[
        \hat \varphi = \Tilde \varphi + \eta \sum_{i = 1}^m g_i
    \]
    for sufficiently small $\eta$. It is known that a sufficiently small perturbation of a Morse function does not change the number of critical points it has,\footnote{
                                                                                      
    This fact is a corollary of the Stack of records theorem (Exercise 1.4.7 in \cite{GP}) applied to $F: \Omega \times S^1 \to \mathbb R^d\times S^1$ given by $F(x, t) = (\nabla(\Tilde \varphi + \sin(2\pi t) g)(x), t)$.} but we have chosen the $g_i$ such that any critical point of $\Tilde \varphi$ is also a critical point of $\hat \varphi$, and so the critical points of $\hat \varphi$ are the same as $\Tilde \varphi$ for sufficiently small $\eta$. Then \eqref{eq:fixingfunction} ensures that $\hat\varphi \in G$.
<!-- end proof-22 -->
<!-- definition-2: body_md -->
\label{def:AnisotropicBesov}
    For $s > 0$, fix a smoothness $S$ so that $\phi^{\operatorname{bc}}_{lk}, \psi^{\operatorname{bc}}_{lk} \in C^r$ for a natural number $r> s$. We define the Besov spaces
    \[
        B^{s, \operatorname{an}}_{pq} = \{ f \in L^p([0, 1]\times \Omega) : \|f\|_{B^{s, \operatorname{an}}_{pq}}<\infty\},
    \]
    where
    \[
        \|f\|_{B^{s, \operatorname{an}}_{pq}} = \left( \sum_{j = L_0-1}^\infty2^{qj(s+\frac{d+2}{2}-\frac{d+2}{p})}\bigg( \sum_{\substack{\mathbb L(l)= j\\ k \in \mathcal K(l)}} |\langle f, \Psi^{\operatorname{an}}_{lk}\rangle|^p\bigg)^{q/p} \right)^{1/q},
    \]
    with the usual adjustments when $p$ or $q = \infty$.
    In the special case where $p = q = 2$, we note 
    \[
        B^{s, \operatorname{an}}_{22} = H^{s,\operatorname{an}}.
    \]
    The definition is also valid for $s = 0$, $p = \infty$, and $q = 1$.
<!-- end definition-2 -->
<!-- definition-3: body_md -->
Let $p \in [1, \infty]$. Then, we say that $f\in L^p(Q)$ has \textit{finite wavelet support} if 
    \[\langle f, \Psi^{\operatorname{an}}_{lk}\rangle = 0\]
    for all but finitely many indices $l, k$.
<!-- end definition-3 -->
<!-- proposition-18: body_md -->
\label{prop:BesovEmbeddings2}
   The usual embeddings between Besov spaces hold, where the effective dimension is $d+2$. For example, if $s > (d+2)(1/p-1/r)$, with $1\leq p, r\leq \infty$, 
   \[
       B^{s, \operatorname{an}}_{pq} \hookrightarrow L^r([0, 1]\times \Omega).
   \]
<!-- end proposition-18 -->
<!-- proof-23: body_md -->
We only use the cases when $p = q =2$, $s>(d+2)/2$, and $p = \infty$, $q = 1$, $s = 0$, so we will only prove these here. 
    See \textsection 5.3.2 in \cite{T} for the case of anisotropy on $\mathbb R^n$ spaces. Suppose that $u\in H^{s, s/2}$ for $s > (d+2)/2$ has finite wavelet support. Then, we can write $u$ as
    \[
        u(t, x) = \sum_{j \geq L_0} \sum_{(\iota, l), k} \langle u, \Psi^{\iota, \operatorname{an}}_{lk}\rangle \Psi^{\iota, \operatorname{an}}_{lk}(t, x),
    \]
    and denote by $u_j$ the projection of $u$ onto $W_{j-1}$ for $j > L_0$ and onto $V_{L_0}$ for $j = L_0$. We use Cauchy-Schwarz to obtain
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\begin{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
        |u(t, x)| &\leq \sum_{j \geq L_0} \sum_{(\iota, l), k} |\langle u, \Psi^{\iota, \operatorname{an}}_{lk} \rangle| |\Psi^{\iota, \operatorname{an}}_{lk}(t, x)| \leq \sum_{j \geq L_0} \|u_j\|_{L^2}\left(\sum_{(\iota, l), k} |\Psi^{\iota, \operatorname{an}}_{lk}(t, x)|^2\right)^{1/2}.
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\end{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
    Now, by standard wavelet theory one can show that
    \[
        \sum_{(\iota, l), k} |\Psi^{\iota, \operatorname{an}}_{lk}(t, x)|^2 = O(2^{(d+2)j}),
    \]
    so again by Cauchy-Schwarz we have
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\begin{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
        |u(t, x)| &\leq \sum_{j \geq L_0} 2^{(d+2)j/2}\|u_j\|_{L^2} \leq \left(\sum_{j \geq L_0} 2^{(d+2 - 2s)j} \right)^{1/2} \left(\sum_{j \geq L_0} 2^{2js}\|u_j\|^2_{L^2}\right)^{1/2} = C\|u\|_{H^{s, s/2}},
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\end{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
    and we apply the usual extension arguments to obtain the result. The proof is similar for the $s = 0$, $p = \infty$, and $q = 1$ case.
<!-- end proof-23 -->
<!-- remark-16: body_md -->
\label{rmk:equivalentnorm}
    In light of \eqref{eq:atomicdecomp}, we see that indeed any choice of bases for the $W_{j}^{\operatorname{an}}$ and $V_{L_0-1}^{\operatorname{an}}$ would lead to an equivalent definition. From this we can deduce that an alternate choice of tensor basis yields an equivalent space. Indeed, for the proofs we sometimes need to consider finer properties of the periodic Meyer wavelets. This aligns with the notation of \cite{T} more closely. Throughout this section, we will write $\phi^{\operatorname{bc}}$ and $\psi^{\operatorname{bc}}$ for the boundary corrected scaling functions and wavelets, and $\phi$ and $\psi$ as the periodized Meyer scaling functions and wavelets. 

    Let $\mathcal I$ be the set of $d+1$-tuples consisting of $0$s and $1$s with not all entries $0$. Next, we define $\mathcal I^{j, \operatorname{an}}\subseteq \mathcal I \times \mathbb N^2$ where $\mathcal I^{L_0, \operatorname{an}} = \{(0, (2L_0, L_0))\}$, and for $j > L_0$, we define $(\iota, l) \in \mathcal I^{j, \operatorname{an}}$ if and only if
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*}

        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
        l_0 &= 2(j-1) \quad \text{if }\iota_0 = 0,\\
        l_0 &= 2(j-1) \text{ or }2j-1 \quad \text{if } \iota_0 = 1,\\
        l_1 &= j-1.
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {

    Then, we define an alternate basis by
    \[\Psi^{\iota, \operatorname{alt}}_{lk}(t, x) = \psi^{\iota_t, \operatorname{bc}}_{l_tk_t}(t)\Psi^{\iota_x}_{l_x k_x}(x),\]
    where $\psi^{0, \operatorname{bc}} = \phi^{\operatorname{bc}}$, $\psi^{1, \operatorname{bc}} = \psi^{\operatorname{bc}}$, $\Psi^{\iota}$ are tensorized periodic wavelets defined as in \textsection 4.3.6 in \cite{GN}, $\iota = (\iota_t, \iota_x)$, $k = (k_t, k_x)\in [2^{l_0}]\times [2^{l_1}]^d\equiv \mathcal K'(l)$. Then, we have
    \[W^{\operatorname{an}}_j = \operatorname{span}(\Psi^{\iota, \operatorname{alt}}_{lk} : (\iota, l)\in \mathcal I^{j+1}, k \in \mathcal K'(l)).\]
    Thus, using \eqref{eq:atomicdecomp} we see that an equivalent norm for $H^{s, \operatorname{an}}$ is
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation}

    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    \label{eq:oldway}
        \|u\|_{H^{s, \operatorname{an}}}^2 = \sum_{j = L_0}^\infty 2^{2js}\sum_{(\iota, l)\in \mathcal I^{j, \operatorname{an}}}\sum_{k \in \mathcal K'(l)} |\langle u, \Psi^{\iota, \operatorname{alt}}_{lk}\rangle|^2,
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {

    and we mainly use this characterization in this section.

<!-- lemma-8: body_md -->
\label{lem:rieszBlocks}
    Let $u\in L^2(Q)$ have finite wavelet support, and $j \geq L_0$. Then,
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@enumerate} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@enumerate} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@enumerate} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@enumerate} 
        {\begin{enumerate}} 
        {\csname pseudo@enumerate\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@enumerate\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@enumerate\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@enumerate\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    [label = (\alph*)]
        \item for any $i = 1, \dots d$, there is a constant independent of $j$ and $u$ such that
            \[
                \bigg\|\bigg(\frac{\partial u}{\partial x_i}\bigg)_j\bigg\|^2_{L^2(Q)} \leq C 2^{2j}\big(\|u_{j-1}\|^2_{L^2(Q)} + \|u_{j}\|^2_{L^2(Q)} + \|u_{j+1}\|^2_{L^2(Q)}\big), \text{ and }
            \]
        \item for any $\alpha \geq 2$, there is a constant independent of $j, j'$ and $u$ such that
            \[
                \bigg\|\frac{d u}{dt}\bigg\|^2_{H^{\alpha - 2, \operatorname{an}}} \leq C \|u\|_{H^{\alpha, \operatorname{an}}}^2,
            \]
            where we pick $S = S(\alpha)$ the smoothness of the boundary corrected Daubechies wavelets is large enough.
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@enumerate} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@enumerate} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@enumerate} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@enumerate} 
        {\end{enumerate}} 
        {\csname pseudo@enumerate\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@enumerate\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@enumerate\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@enumerate\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
    In particular, we have that if $v\in H^{\alpha, \operatorname{an}}$  then
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@enumerate} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@enumerate} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@enumerate} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@enumerate} 
        {\begin{enumerate}}

    } 
        {\csname pseudo@enumerate\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    [label = (\roman*)]
        \item if $\alpha \geq 1$, $\partial v/\partial x_i \in H^{\alpha -1, \operatorname{an}}$, and
        \item if $\alpha \geq 2$, $d v/dt \in H^{\alpha -2, \operatorname{an}}$
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@enumerate} 
        {
