<!-- proof-24: body_md -->
We do the in particular part first. For $(i)$, note that for $u \in H^{\alpha, \operatorname{an}}$ such that $\frac{\partial u}{\partial x_i}$ exists, we can compute using $(a)$
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\begin{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
        \bigg\|\frac{\partial u}{\partial x_i}\bigg\|_{H^{\alpha -1, \operatorname{an}}}^2 &= \sum_{j\geq L_0} 2^{2j(\alpha - 1)}\bigg\|\frac{\partial u}{\partial x_i}\bigg\|_{L^2}^2 \leq 3C \sum_{j \geq L_0}2^{2j\alpha}\|u_j\|_{L^2}^2 = 3C \|u\|_{H^{\alpha, \operatorname{an}}}^2,
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\end{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
    and since such $u$ are dense in $H^{\alpha, \operatorname{an}}$ we can extend via the usual arguments. For $(ii)$ again take $u\in H^{\alpha, \operatorname{an}}$ such that $\frac{d u}{dt}$ exists, and using $(b)$, extend via the usual arguments.

    First, we claim that
    \[
        \bigg(\frac{\partial u}{\partial x_i}\bigg)_j = \bigg(\frac{\partial u_{j-1}}{\partial x_i}\bigg)_j +\bigg(\frac{\partial u_j}{\partial x_i}\bigg)_j+ \bigg(\frac{\partial u_{j+1}}{\partial x_i}\bigg)_j.
    \]
    This is because we have
    \[
        \frac{\partial u}{\partial x_i} = \sum_{j' \geq L_0} \sum_{(\iota, l), k} \langle u, \Psi^{\iota, \operatorname{alt}}_{lk}\rangle \frac{\partial \Psi^{\iota, \operatorname{alt}}_{lk}}{\partial x_i},
    \]
    and we note that for some other $(\iota', l')\in \mathcal I^{j, \operatorname{an}}$ and $k \in \mathcal K(l)$, 
    \[
        \bigg\langle \frac{\partial \Psi^{\iota, \operatorname{alt}}_{lk}}{\partial x_i}, \Psi^{\iota', \operatorname{alt}}_{l'k'}\bigg\rangle = \langle \psi^{\iota_t, \operatorname{bc}}_{l_tk_t}, \psi^{\iota_t', \operatorname{bc}}_{l_t'k_t'}\rangle \bigg\langle \frac{d \psi^{\iota_i}_{j'-1, k_i}}{dx}, \psi^{\iota_i'}_{j-1,k_i'}\bigg\rangle\prod_{\substack{m = 1 \\ m \neq i}}^d\langle \psi^{\iota_m}_{j'-1, k_m}, \psi^{\iota_m'}_{j-1, k_m'}\rangle,
    \]
    so if $|j' - j| > 1$, then this inner product is $0$ since the Meyer wavelets have disjoint Fourier support in that case, and at least one term in the product will have a Meyer wavelet at the higher level. See Theorem 4.2.9 in \cite{GN}.
    Thus, to bound $(\partial_i u)_j$ in $L^2$ we can bound
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\begin{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
        \|(\partial_i u)_j\|_{L^2} &\leq \|(\partial_i u_{j-1})_j\|_{L^2} + \|(\partial_i u_{j})_j\|_{L^2} + \|(\partial_i u_{j+1})_j\|_{L^2} \\
                                   &\leq \|\partial_i u_{j-1}\|_{L^2} + \| \partial_i u_{j}\|_{L^2} + \|\partial_i u_{j+1}\|_{L^2}.
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\end{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
    Thus, to deduce $(a)$, it suffices to prove that for some constant independent of $j$, 
    \[
        \|\partial_i u_{j}\|_{L^2}^2 \leq C2^{2j}\|u_j\|^2_{L^2}.
    \]
    Now, consider the fact that
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\begin{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
        \|\partial_i u_j\|^2_{L^2} = \sum_{(\iota, l), (\iota', l') \in \mathcal I^{j, \operatorname{an}}} \sum_{k, k'} \langle u, \Psi^{\iota, \operatorname{alt}}_{lk}\rangle \langle u, \Psi^{\iota', \operatorname{alt}}_{l'k'} \rangle \langle \partial_i \Psi^{\iota, \operatorname{alt}}_{lk}, \partial_i \Psi^{\iota', \operatorname{alt}}_{l'k'}\rangle.
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\end{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
    So, consider for $(\iota, l), (\iota', l')\in \mathcal I^{j, \operatorname{an}}$ and $k \in \mathcal K'(l)$, $k' \in \mathcal K'(l')$:
    \[
        \bigg\langle \partial_i \Psi^{\iota, \operatorname{alt}}_{lk}, \partial_i \Psi^{\iota', \operatorname{alt}}_{l'k'}\bigg\rangle = \langle \psi^{\iota_t, \operatorname{bc}}_{l_tk_t}, \psi^{\iota_t', \operatorname{bc}}_{l_t'k_t'}\rangle \bigg\langle \frac{d \psi^{\iota_i}_{j-1, k_i}}{dx}, \frac{ d \psi^{\iota_i'}_{j-1,k_i'}}{dx}\bigg\rangle\prod_{\substack{m = 1 \\ m \neq i}}^d\langle \psi^{\iota_m}_{j-1, k_m}, \psi^{\iota_m'}_{j-1, k_m'}\rangle.
    \]
    The only times when this is non-zero is when $(\iota, l) = (\iota', l')$, and $k = k'$ except at the $i$-th index, so we restrict to this case. Now, from standard wavelet theory using the fact that Meyer wavelets and scaling functions are Schwartz, we have the bound
    \[
        \bigg|\bigg\langle \frac{d \psi^{\iota_i}_{j-1, k_i}}{dx}, \frac{ d \psi^{\iota_i'}_{j-1,k_i'}}{dx}\bigg\rangle\bigg| \leq C 2^{2j}\sum_{m\in \mathbb Z} \frac{1}{(1+|k_i - k_i' + m'2^{j-1}|)^2}.
    \]
    Thus, we see that 
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\begin{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
        \|\partial_i u_j \|^2_{L^2} &= \sum_{\substack{(\iota, l) \in \mathcal I^{j, \operatorname{alt}} \\\iota_i'}}\sum_{\substack{k\in \mathcal K'(l)\\ k_i' \in [2^{j-1} -1]}}\langle u, \Psi^{\iota, \operatorname{alt}}_{lk}\rangle \langle u, \Psi^{\iota', \operatorname{alt}}_{l'k'} \rangle \bigg\langle \frac{d \psi^{\iota_i}_{j-1, k_i}}{dx}, \frac{ d \psi^{\iota_i'}_{j-1,k_i'}}{dx}\bigg\rangle \\
                                    &\leq C2^{2j}\sum_{\substack{(\iota, l) \in \mathcal I^{j, \operatorname{alt}}\\ \iota_i'}}\sum_{\substack{k\in \mathcal K'(l)\\ k_i' \in [2^{j-1} -1]}}|\langle u, \Psi^{\iota, \operatorname{alt}}_{lk}\rangle||\langle u, \Psi^{\iota', \operatorname{alt}}_{l'k'} \rangle| \sum_{m\in \mathbb Z} \frac{1}{(1 + |k_i - k_i' + m2^{j-1}|)^2}\\
                                    &\leq C2^{2j}\|u_j\|^2_{L^2},
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\end{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
    where in the final inequality we have used Young's inequality and the fact that
    \[
        \sum_{k'_i \in [2^{j-1} - 1]}\sum_{m\in \mathbb Z}\frac{1}{(1+|k_i - k_i' + m2^{j-1}|)^2} \leq \frac{\pi^2}{3}.
    \]
    Hence, we have established $(a)$. We prove $(ii)$ using a similar idea. However, we will need the original basis for this part. As before, we can write
    \[
    \frac{ du}{dt} = \sum_{j, j' \geq L_0 -1}\sum_{k_t, k_x} \langle u, \Psi^{\operatorname{an}}_{jj'k_tk_x}\rangle \frac{d \Psi^{\operatorname{an}}_{jj'k_tk_x}}{dt}.
    \]
    Thus, if we re-expand this into the alternate basis, we see
    \[
        \frac{d u}{dt} = \sum_{ii'jj'} \sum_{k_tk_t'k_xk_x'} \langle u, \Psi^{\operatorname{an}}_{jj'k_tk_x}\rangle \bigg\langle \frac{d \Psi^{\operatorname{an}}_{jj'kk_tk_x}}{dt}, \Psi^{\operatorname{an}}_{ii'k_t'k_x'} \bigg\rangle \Psi^{\operatorname{an}}_{ii'k_t'k_x'}.
    \]
    Now, if we examine the inner product
    \[
        \bigg\langle \frac{d \Psi^{\operatorname{an}}_{jj'k_tk_x}}{dt}, \Psi^{\operatorname{an}}_{ii'k_t'k_x'} \bigg\rangle = \bigg\langle \frac{d \psi^{\operatorname{bc}}_{jk_t}}{dt}, \psi^{\operatorname{bc}}_{ik_t'}\bigg\rangle \langle \Psi_{j'k_x}, \Psi_{i' k_x'}\rangle,
    \]
    we see that it is zero unless $j' = i'$ and $k_x = k_x'$ (in which case it is $1$). This means the action of the time derivative decouples from space. So, if we wish to show that 
    \[
        \bigg\| \frac{ du}{dt} \bigg\|_{H^{\alpha - 2, \operatorname{an}}}^2 \leq C \|u\|^2_{H^{\alpha, \operatorname{an}}},
    \]
    it's equivalent to show that the matrices from $\ell^2 \to \ell^2$ given by
    \[
        M^{j'}_{(j, k_t), (i, k_t')} = 2^{-\alpha\max(j/2, j')}2^{(\alpha - 2)\max(i/2, j')}\langle \partial_t \psi^{\operatorname{bc}}_{jk_t}, \psi^{\operatorname{bc}}_{ik_t'} \rangle
    \]
    are bounded uniformly in $j'$. For this, we will use a discrete version of Schur's test, see \cite{HS} Theorem 5.2. First, we note that when $j \leq i$, we can use \eqref{eq:BCWaveletsOrthProp} to see that, for $S$ the smoothness of the boundary-corrected Daubechies wavelets and Taylor's theorem, there is a constant depending only on $S$ such that
    \[
        |\langle \partial_t \psi^{\operatorname{bc}}_{jk_t}, \psi_{i k_t'}^{\operatorname{bc}}\rangle| \leq C 2^{j}2^{-(S-1)(i-j)} = C2^{j}2^{-(S-1)|i-j|}.
    \]
    Then, we see that for $2j' \leq j \leq i$,
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        {\begin{align}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
        |M^{j'}_{(j, k_t), (i, k_t')}| &\leq C2^{-\alpha\max(j/2, j')}2^{(\alpha - 2)\max(i/2, j')}2^{j}2^{-(S-1)|i-j|}\leq C2^{(-S+\alpha/2)|i-j|}.\label{eq:schurtailbound}
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        {\end{align}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
    Now, since we have an eye towards using Schur's test, for a fixed $(j, k_t)$, we want
    \[
        \sum_{(i, k_t')} |M_{(j, k_t), (i, k_t')}^{j'}| \leq C,
    \]
    where $C$ does not depend on $j'$, $j$ or $k_t$. We observe that since the boundary corrected wavelets have compact support, for a fixed $i$, there are only $2^{|i-j|}$ many $k_t'$ such that the supports of $\psi^{\operatorname{bc}}_{jk_t}$ and $\psi^{\operatorname{bc}}_{jk_t'}$ intersect. Hence, by \eqref{eq:schurtailbound}, we get
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {\begin{equation}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    \label{eq:schurtailcase1a}
        \sum_{\substack{(i, k_t') \\ i\geq j}} |M_{(j, k_t), (i, k_t')}^{j'}| \leq C\sum_{i\geq j}  2^{(-S + \alpha/2 + 1)|i-j|}.
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {\end{equation}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
    From this, we now know fix $S$ large enough so that $S > \alpha/2 + 1$. This is the heart of the argument, and we'd ideally like to simply perform the same estimate in the regime where $i \leq j$. However, doing this requires the use of integration by parts. In the case where either $\psi^{\operatorname{bc}}_{jk_t}$ or $\psi^{\operatorname{bc}}_{ik_t'}$ is an internal wavelet, integration by parts is justified, we get the usual estimate
    \[
        |M_{(j, k_t), (i, k_t')}^{j'}| \leq C 2^{-\alpha \max(j/2, j')}2^{(\alpha - 2)\max(i/2, j')}2^{i}2^{-(S-1)|i-j|}.
    \]
    So, if we take a fixed interior index $(j, k_t)$ with $2j' \leq j$, we see
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        {\begin{align}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
        \sum_{\substack{(i, k_t') \\ i\leq j}}|M_{(j, k_t), (i, k_t')}^{j'}| &\leq C\sum_{i\leq j} 2^{|i-j|}2^{-\alpha j/2}2^{(\alpha - 2)\max(i/2, j')}2^{i}2^{-(S-1)|i-j|}\leq C\sum_{i\leq j} 2^{-(S-2)|i-j|} \leq C. \label{eq:schurtailcase1b}
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        {\end{align}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
    Combining \eqref{eq:schurtailcase1a} and \eqref{eq:schurtailcase1b} together we get the needed bound. In the case where both $\psi^{\operatorname{bc}}_{jk_t}$ and $\psi^{\operatorname{bc}}_{ik_t'}$ are boundary wavelets (note that for each $i$ and $j$ there are only $2S^2 = O(1)$ such pairs), this boundary term is unavoidable. But in this case we use an estimate leveraging the weighting factors of in $M$.
Now, consider the case where $(j, k_t)$ is fixed to be a boundary index (say the left boundary for simplicity) and $i\leq j$ with $(i, k_t')$ also a boundary index. Then, we still integrate by parts and obtain
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\begin{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
        |\langle \partial_t \psi^{\operatorname{bc}}_{j k_t}, \psi^{\operatorname{bc}}_{ik_t'}\rangle| &\leq |\psi^{\operatorname{bc}}_{jk_t}(0)\psi^{\operatorname{bc}}_{ik_t'}(0)| + |\langle \psi^{\operatorname{bc}}_{jk_t}, \partial_t \psi^{\operatorname{bc}}_{ik_t'}\rangle| \leq C(2^{(i+j)/2} + 2^{i}2^{-(S-1)|i-j|}).
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\end{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
    Now the second term is well-behaved as we've seen, so it remains to bound
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\begin{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
        \sum_{i\leq j} 2^{-\alpha j/2}&2^{(\alpha - 2)\max(i/2, j')}2^{(i+j)/2} = 2^{-(\alpha -1)j/2}\sum_{i\leq j}2^{(\alpha - 2)\max(i/2, j')}2^{i/2} \\
                                                                               &\leq 2^{-(\alpha - 1)j/2}\left(\sum_{i\leq 2j'}2^{(\alpha -2)j'}2^{i/2} + \sum_{2j' < i \leq j}2^{(\alpha - 1)i/2}\right) \leq C,
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\end{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
    as required. This handles shows the maximum row sum is bounded in the case where $(j, k_t)$ is such that $2j'\leq j$. The remaining cases, i.e. 
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@itemize} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@itemize} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@itemize} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@itemize} 
        {\begin{itemize}} 
        {\csname pseudo@itemize\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@itemize\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@itemize\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@itemize\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
        \item the maximum row sum is bounded when $(j, k_t)$ is such that $j \leq 2j'$,
        \item the maximum column sum is bounded when $(i, k_t')$ is such that $2j' \leq i$, and
        \item the maximum column sum is bounded when $(i, k_t')$ is such that $2j' \geq i$,
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@itemize} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@itemize} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@itemize} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@itemize} 
        {\end{itemize}} 
        {\csname pseudo@itemize\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@itemize\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@itemize\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@itemize\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
    follow from the same techniques, and we won't do them here. Note that we require $\alpha \geq 2$ because in the case where $(i, k_t')$ is fixed, and $2j' \leq i \leq j$, we need the sum (coming from the boundary wavelets)
    \[
        \sum_{i\leq j} 2^{-\alpha j/2}2^{(\alpha -2)i/2}2^{(i+j)/2} = \sum_{i \leq j}2^{-(\alpha - 1)(j-i)/2}
    \]
    to converge (so indeed any $\alpha > 1$ would do).
<!-- end proof-24 -->
<!-- proposition--2: body_md -->
Let $m\in \mathbb N$, then 
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*}

        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
        \bigcap_{k=0}^{m} H^k([0, 1], H^{2m-2k}(\Omega)) &= H^{2m, \operatorname{an}},\\
        \bigcap_{k = 0}^{m+1} H^k([0, 1], H^{2m+1 - 2k}(\Omega)) &\subseteq H^{2m+1, \operatorname{an}},
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\end{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
    where $\subseteq$ indicates a continuous embedding, and equality indicates equivalent norms. In particular, we have $H^{s, \operatorname{an}} = H^{s, s/2}$ for $s\geq 0$.
<!-- end proposition--2 -->
<!-- remark-17: body_md -->
We note that one containment being continuous, and the equality as sets automatically implies the norms are equivalent by standard functional analysis. See Remark 4.3.1 in \cite{GN}.
<!-- end remark-17 -->
<!-- proof-25: body_md -->
Firstly, let $X = \bigcap H^k([0, 1], H^{2m-2k}(\Omega))$, and let $Q = [0, 1]\times \Omega$.  We will first show that $H^{2m, \operatorname{an}} \subseteq X$, and then $X\subseteq H^{2m, \operatorname{an}}$.

    Let $f \in H^{2m, \operatorname{an}}$. Then to show that $f \in X$ it suffices to show that $D_t^bD_x^\beta f \in L^2(Q)$, where $2b + |\beta| \leq 2m$. In our setting, what we mean by this is that for any function $\chi \in C_c((0, 1)\times \Omega)$, we have
    \[
        \int_{Q} D^b_tD^\beta_x f (t, x) \chi(t, x)\, dtdx = (-1)^{b+ |\beta|}\int_{Q}f(t, x) D^b_tD^\beta_x \chi (t, x)\, dtdx.
    \]
    Then, we use Lemma \ref{lem:rieszBlocks} to see that indeed $f\in X$. 
                                                      
       
                                                                                                                                                                 
       
                                                                       
                                             
                                                                                                                               
                   
                                                                
       
                                                                                                                       
       
                     
       
                                                                                                               
       
                                                                               
       
                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                       
       
                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                         
    
    Next, let $f \in X$. Then we wish to show that $f \in H^{2m, \operatorname{an}}$, i.e.
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {\begin{equation}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    \label{eq:besovNormToBound}
        \sum_{j > L_0} 2^{2j(2m)}\sum_{(\iota, l), k} |\langle f, \Psi_{lk}^{\iota, \operatorname{alt}}\rangle|^2 < \infty
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {\end{equation}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
    Now, since $f \in L^2([0, 1], H^{2m}(\Omega))$, we know that $f(t, \cdot) \in H^{2m}(\Omega)$ for almost every $t$. Thus, by the wavelet characterization of $H^{2m}(\Omega)$ (see \textsection 4.3.6 in \cite{GN}), we get the following. See Theorem 2.55 in \cite{T2} for the corresponding wavelet characterization of $H^{m}([0, 1])$.
    \[
        \langle f(t, \cdot), \Psi^{\iota_x}_{j k_x}\rangle = 2^{-j(2m)}a^{\iota_x}_{jk_x}(t)
    \]
    for some sequence of functions $a^{\iota_x}_{jk_x}$ such that 
    \[
        \sum_{j = 0}^\infty \sum_{\substack{\iota_x\in \mathcal I\\ k_x\in [2^{j}-1]^d}} |a^{\iota_x}_{j k_x}(t)|^2 < \infty \quad \text{ for almost every }t.
    \]
    But we again observe from the wavelet characterization that 
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\begin{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
        C \|f\|_{L^2([0, 1], H^{2m}(\Omega))}^2 &\geq \int_{[0, 1]} C\|f(t, \cdot)\|^2_{H^{2m}(\Omega)} \, dt \geq \int_{[0, 1]}\sum_{j = 0}^\infty \sum_{\substack{\iota_x\in\mathcal I \\ k_x \in [2^j-1]^d}} |a^{\iota_x}_{jk_x}(t)|^2 \, dt\\
                                                  &\geq \sum_{j = 0}^\infty \sum_{\substack{\iota_x\in\mathcal I \\ k_x \in [2^j-1]^d}} \int_{[0, 1]}|a^{\iota_x}_{jk_x}(t)|^2 \, dt.
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\end{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
Thus, if we now consider
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\begin{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
    \sum_{j >L_0} \sum_{\substack{(\iota, l) \in \mathcal I^{j, \operatorname{an}}\\ k \in \mathcal K(l)\\ \iota_x \neq 0}} 2^{2j(2m)} |\langle f, \Psi^{\iota, \operatorname{alt}}_{lk} \rangle |^2 &= \sum_{j >L_0} \sum_{\substack{(\iota, l) \in \mathcal I^{j, \operatorname{an}}\\ k \in \mathcal K(l)\\ \iota_x \neq 0}} 2^{2j(2m)} |\langle \langle f, \Psi^{\iota_x}_{l_1k_x}\rangle, \psi^{\iota_t}_{l_0k_t} \rangle |^2 \\
    &= \sum_{j >L_0} \sum_{\substack{(\iota, l) \in \mathcal I^{j, \operatorname{an}}\\ k \in \mathcal K(l)\\ \iota_x \neq 0}} |\langle a^{\iota_x}_{l_1k_x}, \psi^{\iota_t}_{l_0k_t} \rangle |^2.\\
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\end{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
\vspace{-15mm}

But for a fixed $(\iota_t, l_0)$, the collection $(\psi^{\iota_t, \operatorname{bc}}_{l_0k_t})$ for $k_t \in [2^{l_0}-1]$ is orthonormal, and there are only three possibilities for $(\iota_t, l_0)$, namely $(0, 2(j-1)$, $(1, 2(j-1))$, or $(1, 2j-1)$, so
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\begin{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
    \sum_{j >L_0} \sum_{\substack{(\iota, l) \in \mathcal I^{j, \operatorname{an}}\\ k \in \mathcal K(l)\\ \iota_x \neq 0}} 2^{2j(2m)} |\langle f, \Psi^{\iota, \operatorname{alt}}_{lk} \rangle |^2 \leq 3 \sum_{j >L_0} \sum_{\substack{\iota_x \in \mathcal I\\ k_x \in [2^{j-1}-1]}} \|a^{\iota_x}_{j-1, k_x}\|^2_{L^2([0, 1])}\leq 3C\|f\|^2_{L^2([0, 1], H^{2m}(\Omega))}.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\end{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
This is nearly the complete Besov space norm. However, we are missing the summands where $(\iota, l)\in \mathcal I^{j, \operatorname{an}}$ satisfies $\iota_x = 0$. Note that in such a case, $\iota_t = 1$. To handle this, we will use the wavelet characterization of Sobolev spaces on the interval, see Theorem 2.55 in \cite{T2}. We know that $f \in H^{m}([0, 1], L^2(\Omega))$, and again from Fubini and the fact wavelets are $L^\infty$ functions, we deduce that $\langle f, \Psi^{\iota_x}_{j k_x}\rangle_\Omega \in H^{m}([0, 1])$, with 
    \[
        D^b_t \langle f, \Psi^{\iota_x}_{j k_x} \rangle_\Omega = \langle D^b_t f, \Psi^{\iota_x}_{j k_x} \rangle_\Omega
    \]
    Now, if we define the functions 
    \[
        a^{\iota_x}_{jk_x}(t) = \langle f, \Psi^{\iota_x}_{jk_x}\rangle,
    \]
    then as stated earlier, $a^{\iota_x}_{j k_x} \in H^m([0, 1])$, and moreover for $b \leq m$,
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\begin{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
        \sum_{j = 0}^\infty \sum_{\substack{ \iota_x \in \mathcal I \\ k_x \in [2^j-1]^d}} \| D^b_t a^{\iota_x}_{j k_x}\|^2_{L^2([0, 1])} &= \sum_{j = 0}^\infty \sum_{\substack{\iota_x \in \mathcal I \\ k_x \in [2^j-1]^d}} \int_0^1 |\langle D^b_t f, \Psi_{jk_x}^{\iota_x} \rangle_\Omega |^2 \, dt = \int_0^1 \| D^b_t f \|^2_{L^2(\Omega)} \, dt.
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\end{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
    Hence, we deduce
    \[
        \sum_{j = 0}^\infty \sum_{\substack{ \iota_x \in \mathcal I \\ k_x \in [2^j-1]^d}} \|a^{\iota_x}_{j k_x}\|^2_{H^{m}([0, 1])} = \|f\|^2_{H^{m}([0, 1], L^2(\Omega))} < \infty.
    \]
    Next, note that we are trying to show that the terms in the Besov norm with $\iota = (1, 0)$ are finite, i.e.
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\begin{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
        \sum_{j >L_0} \sum_{k_x \in [2^{j-1}-1]^d} 2^{2j(2m)}\Big( &\sum_{k_t \in [2^{2(j-1)}-1]}|\langle f, \Psi^{(1, 0), \operatorname{alt}}_{(2(j-1), j-1), (k_t, k_x)}\rangle|^2  \\
                                                                     &+\quad \sum_{k_t \in [2^{2j-1}-1]} |\langle f, \Psi^{(0,1), \operatorname{alt}}_{(2j-1, j-1), (k_t, k_x)}\rangle|^2\Big) \\
            = \sum_{j >L_0} \sum_{k_x \in [2^{j-1}-1]^d} 2^{2j(2m)}\Big( &\sum_{k_t \in [2^{2(j-1)}-1]}|\langle \langle f, \psi^{bc}_{2(j-1), k_t}\rangle, \Psi^{0}_{j-1, k_x}\rangle|^2  \\
                                                                     &+\quad \sum_{k_t \in [2^{2j-1}-1]} |\langle \langle f, \psi^{bc}_{2j-1, k_t}\rangle, \Psi^{0}_{j-1, k_x}\rangle|^2 \Big).
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\end{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
    But, here we note that the collection $(\Psi^0_{j-1, k_x})$ for $k_x \in [2^{j-1}-1]^d$ is an orthonormal basis of $\mathbf V_{j-1}$ from \textsection \ref{sec:WaveletFacts}, so the above sum equals
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\begin{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
        =\sum_{j >L_0} \sum_{l \leq j-1}\sum_{\substack{\iota_x \in \mathcal I \\ k_x \in [2^{l}-1]^d}} 2^{2j(2m)}\Big( &\sum_{k_t \in [2^{2(j-1)}-1]}|\langle a^{\iota_x}_{lk_x}, \psi^{bc}_{2(j-1), k_t}\rangle|^2  \\
                                                                                                                          &+\quad \sum_{k_t \in [2^{2j-1}-1]} |\langle a^{\iota_x}_{lk_x}, \psi^{\operatorname{bc}}_{2j-1, k_t}\rangle|^2 \Big) \\
        \leq  \sum_{l =0}^\infty\sum_{\substack{\iota_x \in \mathcal I \\ k_x \in [2^{l}-1]^d}} \sum_{j >L_0}2^{2j(2m)}\Big( &\sum_{k_t \in [2^{2(j-1)}-1]}|\langle a^{\iota_x}_{lk_x}, \psi^{bc}_{2(j-1), k_t}\rangle|^2  \\
                                                                                                                          &+\quad \sum_{k_t \in [2^{2j-1}-1]} |\langle a^{\iota_x}_{lk_x}, \psi^{\operatorname{bc}}_{2j-1, k_t}\rangle|^2 \Big) \\
            \leq C \sum_{l = 0}^\infty \sum_{\substack{\iota_x \in \mathcal I \\ k_x \in [2^l-1]^d}} \| a^{\iota_x}_{lk_x}\|^2_{H^{m}([0, 1])} &= C\|f\|^2_{H^m([0, 1], L^2(\Omega))},
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\end{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
    where the last inequality follows from the wavelet characterization of the space $H^{m}([0, 1])$. Thus, we have shown that $f \in H^{2m, \operatorname{an}}$, (and $\|\cdot\|_{H^{2m, \operatorname{an}}} \lesssim \|\cdot \|_X$), so we are done. The argument is the same for the $2m+1$ case. The in particular part follows from an interpolation argument. For any $s\geq 0$, let $m\in \mathbb N$ be such that $2m > s$. Then, from Proposition 2.1 in \cite{LM2} we have for $2m(1-\theta) = s$
    \[
        [H^{2m, m}, L^2]_{\theta} = H^{s, s/2}.
    \]
    But we also have
    \[
        [H^{2m, \operatorname{an}}, L^2]_{\theta} = H^{s, \operatorname{an}},
    \]
    so indeed these spaces must coincide. To see the interpolation result for the wavelet spaces, consider the definition of interpolation given in \cite{LM} Definition 2.1: The $\Lambda$ operator is given by
    \[
        \Lambda(u) = \sum_{j \geq L_0} \sum_{(\iota, l), k} 2^{2mj}\langle u, \Psi^{\iota, \operatorname{alt}}_{lk}\rangle\Psi^{\iota, \operatorname{alt}}_{lk}.
    \]
    It is diagonal with respect to the anisotropic basis, and so
    \[
        \Lambda^{1-\theta}(u) =\sum_{j \geq L_0} \sum_{(\iota, l), k} 2^{2m(1-\theta)j}\langle u, \Psi^{\iota, \operatorname{alt}}_{lk}\rangle\Psi^{\iota, \operatorname{alt}}_{lk}.
    \]
<!-- end proof-25 -->
<!-- proposition-19: body_md -->
\label{prop:EigenSobolevChar}
    Assume that $\| D \|_{H^s(\Omega)} \leq U$ for some $s > d$. Then we have for $k \leq s+1$, 
    \[
        \bar H^k_{D}(\Omega) = \dot H^k(\Omega) \quad \text{ and } \| \phi\|_{H^k} \simeq \|\phi\|_{\bar H^k_D}.
    \]
<!-- end proposition-19 -->
<!-- proposition-20: body_md -->
\label{prop:LinearParabolicRegularity}
    Suppose $\theta \in H^1(\Omega)$, $h \in L^2([0, S], L^2(\Omega))$, $a\in L^\infty(Q)$, and $D: \Omega 
    \to [D_{\min}, \infty)$, $D_{\min} >0$, $D \in C^1(\Omega)$. Then there exists a unique strong solution of \eqref{eq:LinearParabolicEq} $u$ satisfying
    \[
        u \in L^2([0, S], H^2(\Omega))\cap C([0, S], H^1(\Omega)), \quad u' \in L^2([0, S), L^2(\Omega)), 
    \]
    and we have the estimates
    \[
        \|u\|_{L^2([0, S], H^2(\Omega)} + \|u'\|_{L^2([0, S], L^2(\Omega))}\leq C (\| h\|_{L^2([0, S], L^2(\Omega))} + \| \theta\|_{H^1(\Omega)})
    \]
    and
    \[
        \|u\|_{L^2([0, S], H^1(\Omega)} + \|u'\|_{L^2([0, S], H^{-1}(\Omega))}\leq C (\| h\|_{L^2([0, S], H^{-1}(\Omega))} + \| \theta\|_{L^2(\Omega)}),
    \]
    where $C$ depends only on an upper bound on the $C^1$-norm of $D$, $D_{\min}$, $d$, $\|a\|_\infty$, and $S$.
<!-- end proposition-20 -->
<!-- theorem-8: body_md -->
\label{thm:strongsolsHRDE}
    For $f\in C_c^\infty(\mathbb R)$, $D \in C^1(\Omega)$, and $\theta \in H^1(\Omega)$, $d \in \mathbb N$, there exists a strong solution $u = u_{\theta, D}$ to the heterogeneous reaction-diffusion equation \eqref{eq:HRDE} that is unique in $C([0, 1], L^2(\Omega))$.
<!-- end theorem-8 -->
<!-- proof-26: body_md -->
This proof of existence adapts ideas from \textsection 3.1.1 in \cite{N26+} and p.537f in \cite{Evans}.
    Define the Banach space $X \equiv C([0, S], L^2(\Omega))$, for some $S >0$ to be chosen, and imbue it with the norm
    \[
        \| u\|_X = \sup_{0 < t < S} \| u(t) \|_{L^2(\Omega)}.
    \]
    For any $u \in X$ define a new function $h(t, \cdot) = f(u(t, \cdot)) \in L^\infty([0, S], L^\infty(\Omega)$. The \textit{linear} parabolic PDE
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\begin{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
        \frac{\partial w}{\partial t} + T_Dw &= h \quad \text{ on } (0, S]\times \Omega, \\
        w(0, \cdot) &= \theta \quad \text{ on }\Omega,
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\end{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
    has a unique strong solution $w \in L^2([0, S], H^2(\Omega))\cap C([0, S], H^1(\Omega))$ with $w' \in L^2([0, S], L^2(\Omega))$ by Proposition \ref{prop:LinearParabolicRegularity}. For $u_i \in X$, $h_i = f(u_i)$, $i = 1, 2$, consider solutions $w_i \equiv A(u_i) \in X$ to \eqref{eq:LinearParabolicEq} and define the constant
    \[
        i_{1, 2} = \int_\Omega (w_1 - w_2) = \langle w_1 - w_2, 1\rangle_{L^2(\Omega)}.
    \]
    We can test the equation \eqref{eq:LinearParabolicEq} against $1$ and get that
    \[
        \frac{d}{dt}\langle w_1 - w_2, 1 \rangle + \langle T_D (w_1 - w_2), 1\rangle = \langle f(u_1) - f(u_2), 1\rangle.
    \]
    Now, we can use divergence theorem to show the middle term vanishes. If we integrate from $0$ to $t$, we see
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\begin{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
        |i_{1, 2}(t)| = |i_{1, 2}(t) - i_{1, 2}(0)| = \left|\int_0^t\int_\Omega (f(u_1(s, x)) - f(u_2(s, x))) \, dxds\right| \\
        \leq S\sup_{0 < t < S} \| f(u_1) - f(u_2) \|_{L^2(\Omega)} \leq S\| f\|_{\operatorname{lip}}\|u_1 - u_2 \|_X.
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\end{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
    Testing \eqref{eq:LinearParabolicEq} for $w_1$ and $w_2$ against $w_1 - w_2$, and then subtracting them, we get
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\begin{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
        \frac{d}{dt}\|w_1 - w_2\|^2_{L^2(\Omega)} &+ 2D_{\min} \| \nabla (w_1 - w_2 - i_{1, 2}) \|_{L^2}^2 \\
                                                  &\leq \frac{d}{dt}\|w_1 - w_2\|^2_{L^2(\Omega)} + 2\| \sqrt D \nabla(w_1-w_2-i_{1, 2})\|^2_{L^2} \\
                                                  &= 2 \langle w_1 - w_2, h_1 - h_2\rangle_{L^2(\Omega)}\\
                                                  & \leq (\epsilon/2)\|w_1 - w_2 - i_{1, 2} + i_{1, 2} \|^2_{L^2(\Omega)} + \frac{2}{\epsilon} \| f(u_1) - f(u_2) \|^2_{L^2(\Omega)} \\
                                                  &\leq \epsilon \| \nabla(w_1 - w_2 - i_{1, 2}) \|^2_{L^2(\Omega)} + (S^2 + \frac{2}{\epsilon})\|f\|_{\operatorname{lip}}^2\|u_1 - u_2\|_X^2.
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\end{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
    Then we can choose $\epsilon$ small enough and subtracting, we get
    \[
        \frac{d}{dt}\| w_1 - w_2 \|_{L^2(\Omega)}^2 \leq c(S^2 + 1) \| u_1 - u_2 \|_X^2,
    \]
    for some constant $c = c(\|f\|_{\operatorname{lip}}, D_{\min}, d)$. Integrating from $0$ to $t$, and using the fact that $(w_1 - w_2)(0) = 0$, we get
    \[
        \| A(u_1) - A(u_2) \|_X^2 = \sup_{0 < t < S} \| w_1(t) - w_2(t) \|^2_{L^2(\Omega)} \leq c(S^2 + 1)S \|u_1 - u_2 \|_X^2.
    \]
    We deduce for some fixed $S_0$ small enough that $A$ is a contraction mapping on $X$, so by the contraction mapping theorem, there exists a unique $u$ satisfying $u = A(u)$ on the interval $[0, S_0]$. Since $u$ is a strong solution to \eqref{eq:HRDE} if and only $u = A(u)$, we obtain the desired result.
<!-- end proof-26 -->
<!-- proposition-21: body_md -->
\label{prop:VVSpatialReg}
    Assume that $D\in C^1(\Omega)$. For $\theta \in H^1(\Omega)$, $D \in H^s(\Omega)$,  and $f \in C^\infty_c(\mathbb R)$, let $u$ be a solution of \eqref{eq:HRDE}. Suppose that $\| \theta \|_{H^\gamma(\Omega)}, \|D\|_{H^\gamma(\Omega)} \leq B$ for $0\leq \gamma \leq s$ and assume that $d \leq 3$. Then we have
    \[
        \int_0^S \|u_{\theta, D}(t)\|^2_{H^{\gamma+1}(\Omega)} \, dt + \sup_{0\leq t \leq S} \| u_{\theta, D} \|_{H^{\gamma}(\Omega)} \leq C, 
    \]
    for some $C = C(B, f, \gamma, d, D_{\min}, \|D\|_{C^1}, S)$, and writing $u' = (\partial u/\partial t)$, we also have
    \[
        \int_0^S \|u'_{\theta, D}(t) \|^2_{H^{\gamma -1 }} \, dt + \operatorname{ess} \sup_{0 \leq t \leq S} \|u'_{\theta, D}(t) \|_{H^{\gamma - 2}} \leq C'.
    \]
    In particular, we have $u_{\theta, D} \in C([0, S], H^\gamma).$
<!-- end proposition-21 -->
<!-- proof-27: body_md -->
We proceed by the usual arguments, where we assume that the solutions $u$ are sufficiently regular and establish a-priori estimates. The general case follows from going back to a Galerkin approximation and then using uniqueness of strong solutions, as is standard.

    \textbf{Step 1: Preliminary identities and estimates.} Start with equation \eqref{eq:HRDE}, and test it against $T_D^{\gamma} u$. Then, we get
    \[
        \frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt} \langle u, T_D^{\gamma} u \rangle + \langle T_D u, T_D^{\gamma} u \rangle = \langle f(u), T_D^{\gamma} u \rangle.
    \]
    Using the fact that $T_D$ is symmetric, we observe that
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {\begin{equation}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    \label{eq:Generalgammacase}
        \frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt} \| T_D^{\gamma/2} u \|^2_{L^2} + \| T_D^{(\gamma + 1)/2} u \|^2_{L^2}= \langle T_D^{(\gamma -1)/2}f(u), T_D^{(\gamma + 1)/2} u \rangle.
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {\end{equation}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
    For the case $\gamma = 0$, we get
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {\begin{equation}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    \label{eq:0gammacase}
        \frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt} \|u(t)\|_{L^2(\Omega)}^2 + \| \sqrt D \nabla u \|_{L^2(\Omega)}^2 = \langle f(u), u \rangle.
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {\end{equation}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
    Since $f(u)$ is uniformly bounded and of compact support, we know that
    \[
        \langle f(u), u \rangle_{L^2} = \int_\Omega f(u) u \mathbbm{1}_{|u|\leq K} \leq c_f < \infty, \quad \text{ for some }K > 0. 
    \]
    So, then going back to \eqref{eq:0gammacase}, we get
    \[
        \frac{d}{dt} \| u(t) \|_{L^2}^2 + 2 \| \sqrt D \nabla u \|_{L^2}^2 \leq 2c_f,
    \]
    which by integrating we obtain $\sup_{0 \leq t \leq S} \| u(t) \|_{L^2}^2 \leq 2Sc_f + \|\theta\|_{L^2}^2< \infty$ as well as 
    \[
        \int_0^S \| \nabla u \|^2_{L^2} \, dt \leq \frac{Sc_f}{D_{\min}} + \frac{1}{D_{\min}}\|u(0)\|^2_{L^2} \leq C(B, f, S, D_{\min}).
    \]
    Since $\|u\|^2_{H^1} \simeq \| u \|^2_{L^2} + \| \nabla u \|^2_{L^2}$ this proves the proposition in the case $\gamma = 0$.

    Next, we note that from the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, Proposition \ref{prop:EigenSobolevChar}, and Young's inequality
    \[
        \langle T_D^{\gamma - 1/2} u, T_D^{\gamma + 1/2} u \rangle \lesssim \|f(u)\|_{H^{\gamma - 1}} \|T_D^{\gamma+1/2}u\|_{L^2} \leq 2\|f(u) \|_{H^{\gamma - 1}}^2+ \frac{1}{4}\| T^{\gamma + 1/2}_D u \|_{L^2}^2.
    \]
    Thus, returning to \eqref{eq:Generalgammacase}, we see
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {\begin{equation}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    \label{eq:GronwallTypeIneq}
        \frac{d}{dt} \|T_D^{\gamma/2}u\|_{L^2}^2 + \|T^{(\gamma + 1)/2}_Du\|^2_{L^2} \lesssim \| f(u) \|_{H^{\gamma - 1}}^2.
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {\end{equation}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
    \textbf{Step 2: Proof of a-priori estimates.} We now want to prove the estimates in the proposition for $\gamma \geq 1$. We argue by induction. Suppose that
    \[
        \int_0^S \|u_{\theta, D}(t)\|^2_{H^{\gamma}(\Omega)} \, dt + \sup_{0\leq t \leq S} \| u_{\theta, D} \|_{H^{\gamma- 1}(\Omega)} \leq C, 
    \]
    In which case we have that
    \[
        \|u(t) \|_{H^{\gamma - 1}} \leq C,
    \]
    with constant $C = C(S, B, D_{\min}, \|D\|_{C^1}, d)$. We show that as a consequence that the RHS of \eqref{eq:GronwallTypeIneq} is bounded by a fixed constant. For $\gamma = 1$, this is follows from $f \in L^\infty$. For $\gamma = 2$, we know
    \[
        \|f(u)\|_{H^1} \lesssim \|f(u)\|_{L^2} + \max_{|\alpha| = 1}\|f'(u)D^\alpha u \|_{L^2} \leq \|f\|_\infty + \|f'\|_\infty\|u\|_{H^1} \leq C.
    \]
    For $\gamma = 3$, we have again that $\|f(u) \|_{H^2} \lesssim \|f\|_\infty + \max_{|\alpha| = 2}\|D^\alpha(f(u))\|_{L^2}$ and need to estimate the last term. We know that
    \[
        D^\alpha (f(u)) = f''(u)D^{\alpha_1} uD^{\alpha_2} u + f'(u)D^\alpha u.
    \]
    The second term is easily bounded by $\|f'\|_\infty \|u\|_{H^2}$, and the first is bounded by
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\begin{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
        \|f''\|_\infty \bigg(\int_\Omega (D^{\alpha_1}u D^{\alpha_2}u)^2\, dx\bigg)^{1/2} \lesssim \| D^{\alpha_1}u \|_{L^4} \|D^{\alpha_2} u \|_{L^4},
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\end{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
    and we note that when $d\leq 4$, that $H^1(\Omega) \hookrightarrow L^4(\Omega)$, and so we get that the above is bounded by $\|u\|_{H^2}^2$. Next, the restriction $d \leq 3$ is required. We have the multiplier inequality for Sobolev norms is
    \[
        \|fg\|_{H^a} \lesssim \|f\|_{H^a} \|g\|_{H^a}
    \]
    for $a > d/2$. Thus, we can apply it in the case of $H^2$-norms when $d\leq 3$. Now, for $\gamma = 4$, we have 
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\begin{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
        \|f(u) \|_{H^3} &\lesssim c + \max_{|\alpha| = 1}\|f'(u)D^\alpha u \|_{H^2} \lesssim c + \|f'(u)\|_{H^2}\max_{|\alpha | = 1} \|D^\alpha u \|_{H^2} \\
                        &\lesssim c + \|u\|_{H^3}^3,
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\end{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
    using the same bound we did in the previous case but now with $f'''$ replacing $f''$. For further $\gamma \geq 5$, we do the same argument as above, utilizing the multiplier inequality for $H^{\gamma - 2}$-norms.

    Thus, we have completed the induction, and we have shown that for all $\gamma$, there exists and constant $c_0$ (depending on the usual constants) such that 
    \[
        \frac{d}{dt} \| T_D^{\gamma} u \|^2_{L^2} + \|T_D^{\gamma + 1/2} u \|^2_{L^2} \leq c_0.
    \]
    To prove the a-priori estimates, we now simply do the usual integration argument, and get that 
    \[
        \|T^\gamma_D u(t)\|_{L^2}^2 + \int_0^S \|T_D^{\gamma + 1/2} u \|_{L^2}^2 \, dt \leq c_1(B, f, \gamma, S, d, D_{\min}, \|D\|_{C^1}).
    \]
    Now, we combine this with the bounds on $\|u(t) \|_{L^2}$, and use Proposition \ref{prop:EigenSobolevChar} to deduce the desired a-priori bound. The derivative a-priori bound follows from the fact
    \[
        \left\|\frac{du}{dt}\right\|_{H^{\gamma-1}}^2 = \| T_D u + f(u)\|_{H^{\gamma-1}}^2 \leq 2\|u\|_{H^{\gamma + 1}}^2 + 2\|f(u)\|^2_{H^{\gamma - 1}}.
    \]
    Now that we have deduced the a-priori estimates, we employ the usual Galerkin arguments with respect to the basis $\{e_{j, D}\}_{j\geq 0}$. Note that basis is required as we need for $T_D$ to preserve $E_J = \operatorname{span} \{ e_{j, D}\}_{j = 0}^J$.
<!-- end proof-27 -->
<!-- proof-28: body_md -->
We will show via induction on $k$ that for $\gamma + 1 - 2k \geq -1$, 
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\begin{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
        &\frac{d^j u}{dt^j} \in L^2([0, S], H^{\gamma + 1 - 2j}(\Omega))\quad \text{ for } j = 0, \dots, k,\\
        &\left\| u^{(j)} \right\|_{L^2([0, S], H^{\gamma +1 - 2j}(\Omega))} \leq C,
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\end{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
    where $C = C(B, f, \gamma, d, D_{min}, \|D\|_{C^1}, S, k)$. We have proven the base case of $k = 1$ by Proposition \ref{prop:VVSpatialReg}. Now, using the standard Galerkin argument, it suffices to establish an a-priori bounds on $u^{(k+1)}$. We know that $u$ satisfies 
    \[
        \frac{ d u}{dt} = \nabla \cdot (D\nabla u ) + f(u), 
    \]
    so by differentiating this identity $k$-times w.r.t to time, we get
    \[
        \frac{d^{k+1} u}{dt^{k+1}} = \nabla \cdot ( D \nabla \frac{d^k u}{dt^k}) + \frac{d^{k+1}}{dt^{k+1}} \big( f(u) \big).
    \]
    Now, we can bound 
    \[
        \|\nabla \cdot (D\nabla u^{(k)})\|_{L^2([0, S], H^{\gamma -1-2k})} \lesssim \| u^{(k)} \|_{L^2([0, S], H^{\gamma + 1 - 2k})},
    \]
    which is in turn bounded by some constant from the induction hypothesis. Now we bound the second term. Using Fa\`a di Bruno's formula, we see
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\begin{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
        \frac{d^{k+1}}{dt^{k+1}}\big[f(u)\big] &= \sum \frac{k!}{m_1!\cdots m_k!} f^{(m_1 + \cdots m_k)}(u)\prod_{j = 1}^k \left(\frac{u^{(j)}}{j!}\right)^{m_j}, \\
        &\quad \text{ where }m_1 + 2m_2 + \cdots + km_k = k.
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\end{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
    Thus, it suffices to bound each term of the sum in $L^2([0, S], H^{\gamma - 1 - 2k})$. Next, by the induction hypothesis we have
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {

    \label{eq:bestspaceforuj}
        u^{(j)} \in C([0, S], H^{\gamma - 2k}(\Omega)) \quad \text{ for }j = 0, \dots k-1,
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation}

    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
    with its norm bounded by some constant times the norms on $u^{(j)}$ and $u^{(j+1)}$ (See theorem 4 of \textsection 5.9.2 in \cite{Evans}).

    Suppose now that $\gamma - 1 - 2k \geq 2$. Then, we know $H^{\gamma - 1 - 2k}$ satisfies a multiplier inequality for $d\leq 3$, so for some non-negative integers $m_1, \dots, m_k$, such that $m_1 + 2m_2 + \cdots + km_k = k$, we have
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        {\begin{align}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
        &\int_0^S \|f^{(m_1 + \cdots m_k)}(u)\prod_{j = 1}^k (u^{(j)})^{m_j}\|_{H^{\gamma - 1 - 2k}}^2 \, dt \nonumber \\
        \lesssim &\int^S_0 \|f^{(m_1 + \cdots m_k)}(u)\|_{H^{\gamma - 1 - 2k}}^2\prod_{j=1}^k \|u^{(j)}\|^{2m_j}_{H^{\gamma - 1-2k}} \, dt.\label{eq:easycasegoal}
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        {\end{align}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
    Now, in the last proposition's proof, we showed that if $\sup_{0\leq t\leq S} \|u\|_{H^a}\leq C$, then $\sup_{0\leq t \leq S}\|f(u)\|_{H^a}\leq c'$. For any choice of $a$ provided $d\leq 3$. But we know from \eqref{eq:bestspaceforuj} that $\sup_{0\leq t \leq S}\|u\|_{H^\gamma}\leq c$, so 
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {\begin{equation}}

    \label{eq:normboundonf}
        \sup_{0\leq t \leq S}\|f^{(m_1 + \cdots + m_k)}(u)\|_{H^{\gamma - 1 -2k}} \leq C.
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {\end{equation}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
    Similarly, we also have for each $j = 1, \dots k-1$,
    \[
        \sup_{0\leq t \leq S} \|u^{(j)}\|_{H^{\gamma - 1- 2k}} \lesssim \sup_{0\leq t \leq S} \|u^{(j)}\|_{H^{\gamma - 2j}} \leq C.
    \]
    Thus, we have established that the RHS of \eqref{eq:easycasegoal} is bounded by a constant in all cases except when $m_k \neq 0$. In this case, $m_1 = \cdots = m_{k-1} = 0$, and $m_k = 1$. Then, we get that (using again \eqref{eq:normboundonf})
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {

    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
        \eqref{eq:easycasegoal} &\lesssim \int_0^S \|u^{(k)}\|_{H^{\gamma - 1 - 2k}}^2 \, dt \\
        &\leq  \|u^{(k)}\|_{L^2([0, S], H^{\gamma +1 - 2k})} \leq C,
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\end{align*}}

    }

    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
    so, we have established the a-priori bound when $\gamma - 1 - 2k \geq 2$.

    Next, suppose that $\gamma - 1 - 2k = 0$. The cases $\gamma - 1 - 2k = 1, -1$ use the same ideas. Then, we want to show that for $m_1 + 2m_2 +\cdots + km_k = 1$, 
    \[
        \int_0^S \|f(u)\prod_{j = 1}^k(u^{(j)})^{m_j}\|^2_{L^2(\Omega)}\, dt\leq C.
    \]
    Since $f$ is bounded, we know
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation}

    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
        \int_0^S \|f(u)\prod_{j = 1}^k(u^{(j)})^{m_j}\|^2_{L^2(\Omega)}\, dt\leq C\int_0^S \int_\Omega \prod_{j = 1}^ku^{(j)}(t, x)^{2m_j}\, dxdt. \label{eq:fullexpansion}
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {\end{equation}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
    Now, since $m_1 + 2m_2 + \cdot + km_k = k$, we know
    \[
        \frac{1}{k/m_1} + \frac{1}{k/2m_2} +\cdots \frac{1}{m_k} = 1.
    \]
    Thus, by generalized H\"older's inequality, we get
    \[
        \eqref{eq:fullexpansion} \leq C\int_0^S\prod_{j = 1}^k \|u^{(j)}\|_{L^{2k/j}(\Omega)}^{2m_j}\, dt.
    \]
    Thus, utilizing \eqref{eq:bestspaceforuj}, we know that $\|u^{(j)}\|_{L^{2k/j}(\Omega)}$ is bounded uniformly in time by some constant provided that $H^{\gamma - 2j}$ embeds into $L^{2k/j}$. In this case, we know $\gamma = 1 + 2k$, so for $j \leq k -1$, $\gamma - 2j \geq 3$. Hence, we know that indeed $H^{\gamma - 2j}$ embeds into $L^\infty$. The case where $m_k \neq 0$ is again similar, so we are done when $\gamma = 1+2k$. Finally, to finish this proof use Proposition \ref{prop:besovIsSobolev}.

<!-- proof-29: body_md -->
The proof proceeds similarly to \textsection \ref{sec:HRDERegTheory}, except we utilize Proposition 3.6 in \cite{KN}, and then differentiate in time as performed in Proposition \ref{prop:HRDEReg}. In this case the computations are easier, as instead of having to apply Fa\'a di Bruno's formula on the non-linearity $f(u)$, we simply have to use the product rule on the $(u\cdot \nabla)u$ term.
<!-- end proof-29 -->
<!-- proof-30: body_md -->
The first part is shown follows from Proposition \ref{prop:NSReg} coupled with Proposition \ref{prop:BesovEmbeddings}. Next, let $M \geq 0$, and suppose that $(\varphi_1, \nu_1), (\varphi_2, \nu_2) \in \Theta$ with $\|\varphi_i\|, \nu_i \leq M$ for $i = 1, 2$. Also, let $u_i$ be the solution to \eqref{eq:introNS} with parameters $(\varphi_i, \nu_i)$. We want to show a forward regularity estimate of the form
    \[
        \sup_{0\leq t \leq 1} \|u_1 - u_2\|_{L^2} \leq K\big(\|\varphi_1-\varphi_2\| + |\nu_1 - \nu_2|\big).
    \]
    Set $w = u_1 - u_2$. Then, subtracting the difference of the two equations and rewriting the bilinear terms akin to p.250 in \cite{R}, we see that $w$ satisfies 
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\begin{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
        \frac{dw}{dt} + \nu_1 A w &= -B(w, u_1) - B(u_2, w) - (\nu_1 - \nu_2)Au_2, \\
        w(0) &= \varphi_1 - \varphi_2.
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\end{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
    Taking inner products with $w$, and noting $\langle B(u_2, w), w\rangle = 0$ (Proposition 9.1 in \cite{R}), we see that
    \[
        \frac{1}{2} \frac{d}{dt} \left[ \|w\|_{L^2}^2\right] + \nu_1\|A^{1/2}w\|_{L^2} = -\langle B(w, u_1), w\rangle - (\nu_1 - \nu_2)\langle Au_2, w\rangle.
    \]
    Then, we use (9.26) in \cite{R}, as well as Young's inequality and the divergence theorem to obtain
    \[
        \frac{1}{2} \frac{d}{dt} \left[ \|w\|_{L^2}^2\right] + \nu_1\|A^{1/2}w\|_{L^2}^2 \leq \frac{\nu_1}{2}\|A^{1/2}w\|^2_{L^2} + \frac{k^2}{\nu_1}\|w\|_{L^2}^2\|u_1\|_{L^2}^2 + |\nu_1 - \nu_2|^2 \frac{\|A^{1/2}u_2\|_{L^2}^2}{\nu_1}.
    \]
    Then we apply Gr\"onwall's inequality to obtain
    \[
        \|w(t)\|^2 \leq \exp\left(\int^t_0\frac{2k^2}{\nu_1}\|u_1(s)\|_{L^2}^2\, ds\right)\left(\|w(0)\|^2_{L^2}+ |\nu_1 - \nu_2|^2 \int_0^t \frac{1}{\nu_1} \|u_2(s)\|^2_{L^2} \, ds\right),
    \]
    but we know that $\|u_i\|_{L^2(Q)} \leq C(M)$ for $i = 1, 2$ by Proposition \ref{prop:NSReg}, and so
    \[
        \sup_{t\in [0, 1]} \|w(t)\|^2_{L^2} \leq C(M, d, \nu_{\min}) \left(\|w(0)\|_{L^2}^2 + |\nu_1 - \nu_2|^2\right).
    \]
    Taking square roots yields the second result.
<!-- end proof-30 -->
